Homotopy classification of 4 4 -manifolds with 3 3 -manifold fundamental group
Jonathan Hillman, Daniel Kasprowski, Mark Powell, Arunima Ray

Abstract
. We give a criterion on a group π \pi and a homomorphism w : π → C 2 w\colon\pi\to C_{2} under which closed 4 4 -manifolds with fundamental group π \pi and orientation character w w are classified up to homotopy equivalence by their quadratic 2 2 -types. We verify the criterion for a large class of 3 3 -manifold groups and orientation characters, in particular for the fundamental group π \pi of any closed, orientable 3 3 -manifold whose finite subgroups are cyclic, provided w w vanishes on every element of π \pi of finite order. We deduce a homeomorphism classification of closed, orientable 4 4 -manifolds with infinite dihedral fundamental group ℤ / 2 ∗ ℤ / 2 \mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2 .



1991 Mathematics Subject Classification

57K40.

1.
Introduction

The study of
4
-manifolds up to homotopy equivalence began with the work of Whitehead and Milnor
[
72
,
56
]
, who gave a full classification in the closed, simply connected case.
An appropriate generalisation of Whitehead and Milnor’s classification to aim for was formulated by Hambleton–Kreck
[
26
]
, as follows.

Question 1.1
.

For which fundamental groups can we classify closed
4
-manifolds up to homotopy equivalence in terms of the quadratic
2
-type?

Smooth structures will play no role here, so we work in the generality of topological
4
-manifolds.
The
quadratic
2
-type

Q(M)
of a closed, connected, based
4
-manifold
M
, with a local orientation at the basepoint, consists of the data

Q(M):=(\pi_{1}(M),\pi_{2}(M),k_{M},w_{1}(M),\lambda_{M}).

Here
\pi_{2}(M)
is considered as a
\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]
-module,
k_{M}\in H^{3}(\pi_{1}(M);\pi_{2}(M))
is the
k
-invariant classifying the Postnikov
2
-type of
M
,
w_{1}(M)\in H^{1}(M;\mathbb{Z}/2)
is the first Stiefel–Whitney class, and
\lambda_{M}\colon\pi_{2}(M)\times\pi_{2}(M)\to\mathbb{Z}[\pi_{1}(M)]
is the equivariant intersection form.
In this article, instead of
w_{1}(M)
we will consider equivalently the orientation character
w_{M}\colon\pi_{1}(M)\to\{\pm 1\}
. An
isomorphism
between the quadratic
2
-types
Q(M)
and
Q(M^{\prime})
of
4
-manifolds
M
and
M^{\prime}
consists of a pair of isomorphisms
g_{i}\colon\pi_{i}(M)\to\pi_{i}(M^{\prime})
for
i=1,2
, that respect the
k
-invariant, and such that
g_{1}
intertwines the orientation characters, and
g_{2}
induces an isomorphism of the intersection forms.

There has been considerable progress on an affirmative answer to
1.1
in special cases, for example certain finite fundamental groups by Hambleton–Kreck
[
26
]
, Kasprowski–Powell–Ruppik
[
42
]
, and Kasprowski–Nicholson–Ruppik
[
39
]
, all in the oriented setting. For geometrically 2-dimensional fundamental groups that satisfy the Farrell–Jones conjecture, Hambleton–Kreck–Teichner
[
25
]
showed that the homotopy classification is determined by the quadratic
2
-type together with the
w_{2}
-type.

A key tool in the articles
[
26
,
42
,
39
]
is a criterion due to Hambleton–Kreck
[
26
]
*Theorem 1.1 (i) for finite fundamental groups, which when satisfied implies that an isomorphism of quadratic
2
-types
\smash{Q(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}Q(M^{\prime})}
is induced by a homotopy equivalence
M\simeq M^{\prime}
. We generalise the Hambleton–Kreck criterion in
Theorem

2.4
. Our new criteria can be applied to all fundamental groups, not just finite groups. In other words, we show that for
4
-manifolds satisfying the conditions in
Theorem

2.4
, the homotopy type is determined by the quadratic
2
-type.

Then we investigate whether the conditions of
Theorem

2.4
are satisfied by
3
-manifold groups
, namely those groups that arise as the fundamental group of some (not necessarily orientable) closed
3
-manifold.
To state our main theorem we introduce the following terminology.

Definition 1.2
.

Let
\pi
be a
3
-manifold group, i.e.
\pi=\pi_{1}(Y)
for some closed
3
-manifold
Y
, and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism (which need not be the orientation character of
Y
).
Suppose that
Y
has prime decomposition
Y_{1}\#\cdots\#Y_{m}
.
We say the pair
(\pi,w)
is
admissible
if
w
vanishes on every element of
\pi
of finite order, and if whenever
\pi_{1}(Y_{i})
contains finite order elements,
Y_{i}
is either a lens space or
S^{1}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}
.

The second condition in the definition means that summands
Y_{i}
with fundamental group a non-cyclic finite
3
-manifold group are inadmissible, as are summands
Y_{i}
, other than
S^{1}\times\mathbb{RP}^{2}
, that contain a two-sided
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
.

Remark 1.3
.

In particular, if
\pi
is the fundamental group of a closed, orientable
3
-manifold whose finite subgroups are cyclic, and
w
vanishes on every element of finite order, then
(\pi,w)
is admissible.

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
\pi
be a
3
-manifold group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism, such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed
4
-manifolds, locally oriented at the basepoints. Suppose that
\pi_{1}(M)
and
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
are both isomorphic to
\pi
, via isomorphisms that pull back
w
to
w_{M}
and
w_{M^{\prime}}
respectively.

Then every isomorphism
\smash{Q(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}Q(M^{\prime})}
between the quadratic
2
-types of
M
and
M^{\prime}
is realised by a homotopy equivalence.
In particular,
M
and
M^{\prime}
are homotopy equivalent if and only if they have isomorphic quadratic
2
-types.
Homotopy equivalences are assumed to be basepoint and local orientation preserving.

Theorem

1.4
includes the homotopy classification for oriented
4
-manifolds with the following fundamental groups.

(1)

Fundamental groups of closed, oriented, aspherical
3
-manifolds, i.e.
COAT groups
.

(2)

Free products of finitely many cyclic groups. In particular, the infinite dihedral group
D_{\infty}\cong\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2
.

(3)

The group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Note that Hambleton–Kreck’s result
[
26
]
*Theorem A already covered finite groups with 4-periodic cohomology, so in particular finite fundamental groups of closed
3
-manifolds.

Theorem

1.4
also includes many nonorientable cases.

The proof of
Theorem

1.4
relies on the following foundational results of Baues–Bleile and Hambleton–Kreck.
Let
f\colon M\to P_{2}(M)
and
f^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to P_{2}(M^{\prime})
be
3
-connected maps to the respective Postnikov
2
-types, and suppose there is a homotopy equivalence
g\colon P_{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\simeq}P_{2}(M^{\prime})
. Let
\pi:=\pi_{1}(P_{2}(M^{\prime}))
, and identify all relevant fundamental groups with
\pi
using
f
,
f^{\prime}
, and
g
. Suppose that there is a homomorphism
w\colon\pi\to\{\pm 1\}
that determines the orientation characters of
M
and
M^{\prime}
. Then by
[
26
]
*Theorem 1.1 and

[
3
]
*Corollary 3.2 (stated below as
Theorem

2.1
) there is a homotopy equivalence
h\colon M\to M^{\prime}
, with
h_{*}=(f^{\prime}_{*})^{-1}\circ g_{*}\circ f_{*}\colon\pi_{i}(M)\to\pi_{i}(M^{\prime})
, for
i=1,2
, if and only if there are
(
twisted
)
fundamental classes
[M]
and
[M^{\prime}]
such that
g_{*}\circ f_{*}([M])=f^{\prime}_{*}([M^{\prime}])\in H_{4}(P_{2}(M^{\prime});\mathbb{Z}^{w})
.

Our conditions from
Theorem

2.4
, if satisfied, imply that an isomorphism
Q(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}Q(M^{\prime})
gives rise to a homotopy equivalence
g\colon P_{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\simeq}P_{2}(M^{\prime})
as above such that
g_{*}\circ f_{*}([M])=f^{\prime}_{*}([M^{\prime}])
, and hence by
Theorem

2.1
to a homotopy equivalence between
M
and
M^{\prime}
.
The majority of the proof of
Theorem

1.4
, which starts in
Section

3
and culminates in
Section

9
, consists of careful verification of the criteria from
Theorem

2.4
.

As another application of our criteria from
Theorem

2.4
,
in
Corollary

10.2
we give a new proof of
[
25
]
*Theorem 5.13. This was part of the proof of the classification of
4
-manifolds with geometrically 2-dimensional fundamental groups from that article.
See
Section

10.1
for details.

Remark 1.5
.

A homotopy classification via
Q(M)
as in
Theorem

1.4
does not hold for all fundamental groups. In particular, while it holds for
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
in the orientable case, the analogous statement does not hold for fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/p
whenever there are non-homotopy equivalent
3
-dimensional lens spaces with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}/p
. To see this consider
S^{1}\times L
and
S^{1}\times L^{\prime}
, where
L
and
L^{\prime}
are lens spaces with
\pi_{1}(L)\cong\mathbb{Z}/p\cong\pi_{1}(L^{\prime})
. Then by considering
\mathbb{Z}
-covers we see that
S^{1}\times L
and
S^{1}\times L^{\prime}
are homotopy equivalent if and only if
L
and
L^{\prime}
are homotopy equivalent. However
\pi_{2}(S^{1}\times L)=0=\pi_{2}(S^{1}\times L^{\prime})
and so
S^{1}\times L
and
S^{1}\times L^{\prime}
have isomorphic quadratic
2
-types, even if
L
and
L^{\prime}
are not homotopy equivalent.

We also check that the criteria from
Theorem

2.4
do not hold in this case. Conditions (
1
), (
3
), and (
4
) hold trivially since
\pi_{2}(S^{1}\times L)
is trivial. More precisely, using the notation from
Theorem

2.4
, we can take
B=B(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/p)
, so in particular
H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/p])=0
, and
\operatorname{Im}\varphi_{B}=\{0\}
. However the images of
[S^{1}\times L]
and
[S^{1}\times L^{\prime}]
in
H_{4}(\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/p;\mathbb{Z})
are distinct, so in particular their difference does not lie in
\operatorname{Im}\varphi_{B}
. Hence condition (
2
) does not hold.

Remark 1.6
.

The restrictions on the orientation character in
Theorem

1.4
are necessary. Even for fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}/2
and nontrivial orientation character, the homotopy type of
M
is not determined by
Q(M)
as can be seen from work of Kim, Kojima, and Raymond
[
47
]
.

Remark 1.7
.

In this paper we use the term
PD_{n}
-complex
to refer to a finite
n
-dimensional Poincaré duality complex. A group
G
is said to be a
PD_{n}
-group
if the classifying space
K(G,1)
is a
PD_{n}
-complex.
The proof of
Theorem

1.4
extends to the case that
\pi
is a free product
*_{i=1}^{s}G_{i}
, where the factors
G_{i}
are either
PD_{3}
-groups or cyclic.
This may be a spurious generalisation, as it remains an open question whether every
PD_{3}
-group is the fundamental group of a closed
3
-manifold.

Note that the finiteness conditions on
PD_{n}
-complexes and
PD_{n}
-groups in the literature sometimes differ. For example the algebraic definition of a
PD_{n}
-group from
[
5
]
does not require that the group be finitely presentable, and for every
n\neq 4
there are examples of such groups that are not finitely presentable by
[
13
]
*Theorem C, and so do not have finite classifying spaces.

1.1.
Stable homeomorphism for COAT and
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
fundamental groups

Recall that
4
-manifolds
M
and
M^{\prime}
are said to be
stably homeomorphic
if there exists some
k\geq 0
such that
M\#k(S^{2}\times S^{2})
is homeomorphic to
M^{\prime}\#k(S^{2}\times S^{2})
.
Since closed, oriented
4
-manifolds with COAT or
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
fundamental group are stably homeomorphic if they are homotopy equivalent and have equal Kirby–Siebenmann invariant
[
37
]
*Corollary 1.6
[
40
]
*Theorem B we obtain the following statement.

Corollary 1.8
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed, oriented
4
-manifolds with
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})
either a COAT group or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
. Then
M
and
M^{\prime}
are orientation preserving stably homeomorphic if and only if they have equal Kirby–Siebenmann invariant and their quadratic
2
-types are stably isomorphic.

Similar stable classification statements for
4
-manifolds with COAT fundamental groups were obtained in
[
37
]
*Theorem 9.1 and
[
22
]
*Theorem B.
The latter result of Hambleton–Hildum covers more 3-dimensional groups than COAT groups, but when restricted to COAT groups their result is covered by
[
37
]
*Theorem 9.1. To compare
[
37
]
*Theorem 9.1 with
Corollary

1.8
note that the former used the
w_{2}
-type, but did not consider the
k
-invariant, whereas in the latter we use the quadratic
2
-type, including the
k
-invariant, but the
w_{2}
-type does not appear.

A stable classification for manifolds with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
was obtained in
[
43
]
*Theorem 1.2 using modified surgery over the normal 1-type
[
51
]
, but using a thoroughly different set of invariants.

1.2.
Homeomorphism classification for
4
-manifolds with infinite dihedral fundamental group

Given a homotopy classification as in
Theorem

1.4
, a natural question is whether this can be upgraded to a homeomorphism classification using surgery theory. The first and most famous instance of this is due to Freedman
[
18
]
; once he established that surgery theory could be applied topologically in dimension four for trivial fundamental groups, he improved Whitehead and Milnor’s homotopy classification
[
72
,
56
]
of closed, simply connected
4
-manifolds to a homeomorphism classification. (Initially, Freedman’s classification was stated for
4
-manifolds that can be smoothed away from a point. However shortly afterwards Quinn showed
[
60
]
that this holds for all connected
4
-manifolds.)

For non-simply-connected
4
-manifolds, a prerequisite for applying surgery theoretic methods is that the fundamental group be
good
, a class of groups that contains finite groups and solvable groups, and is closed under subgroups, quotients, extensions, and colimits
[
17
,
46
]
.

In particular the infinite dihedral group
D_{\infty}:=\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2
is good, since it fits into an extension
0\to\mathbb{Z}\to D_{\infty}\to\mathbb{Z}/2\to 0
. As
\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2\cong\pi_{1}(\mathbb{RP}^{3}\#\mathbb{RP}^{3})
it is one of the groups covered by
Theorem

1.4
, with respect to the trivial orientation character.
In addition the Whitehead group
\Wh(D_{\infty})=0
, since
\Wh(\mathbb{Z}/2)=0
and
\Wh(\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2)=\Wh(\mathbb{Z}/2)\oplus\Wh(\mathbb{Z}/2)
by
[
62
]
, which simplifies the application of surgery theory.
We focus on
D_{\infty}
, obtaining a homeomorphism classification for closed, oriented
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
D_{\infty}
, in terms of the quadratic
2
-type, the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant, and an additional invariant
s(M,\alpha)
. This invariant is due to Kreck, Lück, and Teichner
[
49
]
*Definition 2.1, although we give a slightly different description that applies to topological
4
-manifolds.

Definition 1.9
.

Let
M
be a closed, oriented
4
-manifold and let
\alpha\colon\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}D_{\infty}
be an isomorphism.
If the universal cover
\widetilde{M}
is not spin, then set
s(M,\alpha)=0
.
We assume for the rest of the definition that
\widetilde{M}
is spin. The isomorphism
\alpha
induces a map
f\colon M\to\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\vee\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\simeq\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}\cup[0,1]\cup\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
, which is well-defined up to homotopy. Let
S
be a regular preimage of
\tfrac{1}{2}\in[0,1]
under
f
. Then the inclusion
S\subseteq M
lifts to an inclusion
S\subseteq\widetilde{M}
and the unique spin structure of
\widetilde{M}
induces a spin structure on
S
. Let
N
be a spin
4
-manifold with spin boundary
S
. The submanifold
S\subseteq M
decomposes
M
into two parts, i.e.
M=M_{L}\cup_{S}M_{R}
. We define

s(M,\alpha):=(\sigma(M_{L}\cup_{S}-N)/8+\operatorname{ks}(M_{L}\cup_{S}-N),\sigma(M_{R}\cup_{S}N)/8+\operatorname{ks}(M_{R}\cup_{S}N))\in\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2.

For more details see
[
49
]
*Section 2.
In particular,
s(M,\alpha)
is a stable homeomorphism invariant of
(M,\alpha)
by
[
49
]
*Lemma 2.2.

We can now state the promised homeomorphism classification.

Theorem 1.10
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be closed, oriented
4
-manifolds with isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(M_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}D_{\infty}
, for
i=1,2
. Then
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are orientation preserving homeomorphic over
D_{\infty}
if and only if

(1)

M_{1}
and
M_{2}
have isomorphic quadratic
2
-types over
D_{\infty}
,

(2)

\operatorname{ks}(M_{1})=\operatorname{ks}(M_{2})
, and

(3)

s(M_{1},\alpha_{1})=s(M_{2},\alpha_{2})\in\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2
.

Moreover, if conditions (
2
) and (
3
) hold, then every isomorphism of the quadratic
2
-types over
D_{\infty}

is realised by a homeomorphism
M_{1}\to M_{2}
.

Here, we say that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are
homeomorphic over
D_{\infty}
if there exists a homeomorphism
f\colon M_{1}\to M_{2}
such that
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\circ f
. Similarly an isomorphism of
Q(M_{1})
and
Q(M_{2})
over
D_{\infty}
is an isomorphism of the quadratic
2
-types where the constituent isomorphism
g\colon\pi_{1}(M_{1})\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{1}(M_{2})
intertwines
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
, i.e.
\alpha_{1}=\alpha_{2}\circ g
.

Remark 1.11
.

Let
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
be closed, oriented
4
-manifolds with isomorphisms
\alpha_{i}\colon\pi_{1}(M_{i})\xrightarrow{\cong}D_{\infty}
, for
i=1,2
. Let
x,y\in H^{1}(D_{\infty};\mathbb{Z}/2)
be the elements obtained from pulling back the generator of
H^{1}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}/2)
using the map on group cohomology induced by the standard projections
D_{\infty}=\mathbb{Z}/2*\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
, to the first or second factor respectively.

(i)

Note that condition (
1
) in
Theorem

1.10
implies, by
Theorem

1.4
, that
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are homotopy equivalent over
D_{\infty}
. The Stiefel–Whitney classes are homotopy invariant, which follows from the Wu formulae. Thus
\widetilde{M}_{1}
is spin if and only if
\widetilde{M}_{2}
is spin, and also
w_{2}(M_{1})=\alpha_{1}^{*}(x^{2}+y^{2})
if and only if
w_{2}(M_{2})=\alpha_{2}^{*}(x^{2}+y^{2})
.

(ii)

Of course if the
\widetilde{M}_{i}
are both not spin, then
Theorem

1.10
(
3
) holds automatically by definition of
s(M_{i},\alpha_{i})
.

(iii)

Let
M
be a closed, oriented
4
-manifold and let
\alpha\colon\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}D_{\infty}
be an isomorphism. We will show in
Remark

11.2
that if
\widetilde{M}
is spin and
w_{2}(M)\neq\alpha^{*}(x^{2}+y^{2})
, then
s(M,\alpha)
is determined by the signature
\sigma(M)
and the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant
\operatorname{ks}(M)
. So again
Theorem

1.10
(
3
) holds automatically in such cases as a consequence of conditions (
1
) and (
2
).

(iv)

If each
\widetilde{M_{i}}
is spin and
w_{2}(M_{i})=\alpha_{i}^{*}(x^{2}+y^{2})
, then
Theorem

1.10
(
3
) is an important extra condition. For example, let
E
denotes the unique
S^{2}
-bundle over
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
with orientable but not spin total space. There exists a unique
4
-manifold
\mathop{}\!\star E
, which is homotopy equivalent to
E
, but not (stably) homeomorphic to
E
. Then Teichner
[
68
]
showed that the closed, oriented
4
-manifolds
E\#E
and
\mathop{}\!\star E\#\mathop{}\!\star E
are homotopy equivalent, have vanishing Kirby–Siebenmann invariants, but are not (stably) homeomorphic. See
[
41
]
*Example 5.11 for additional discussion of these examples.

(v)

Let
M
be a closed, oriented
4
-manifold together with an isomorphism
\alpha\colon\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}D_{\infty}
. Let
\beta\colon\mathbb{Z}/2\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
be given by
(a,b)\mapsto a+b
.
Then if
w_{2}(M)=\alpha^{*}(x^{2}+y^{2})
, we have that

\operatorname{ks}(M)=\beta(s(M,\alpha))+\sigma(M)/8.

This follows by Novikov additivity of the signature and additivity of the Kirby–Siebenmann invariant. So the invariants are not independent.

(vi)

Item
(iv)
also shows that the analogue of
Corollary

1.8
does not hold for fundamental group
D_{\infty}
.

(vii)

We would like to remove ‘over
D_{\infty}
’ from the statement of
Theorem

1.10
, but we have not been able to do so.
This would follow if we knew that every closed
4
-manifold
M
with fundamental group
D_{\infty}
admits a homotopy self-equivalence that sends
a\mapsto b
and
b\mapsto a
.

In some cases we can simplify the classification. In
Section

11.4
, specifically in
Proposition

11.8
, we show that in some cases we do not need to control the
k
-invariant, and instead it suffices to control the
w_{2}
-type. A special case is the following corollary, in which we try to minimise the algebraic topological computations needed to apply it.

Corollary 1.12
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed, oriented, smooth
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\pi:=D_{\infty}
and equivariant intersection forms both isomorphic to
H(I\pi)\oplus\lambda
, where
\lambda
is a nonsingular Hermitian form on a stably free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module.
Then
M\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and
M^{\prime}\#\mathbb{CP}^{2}
are homeomorphic, as are
M\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
and
M^{\prime}\#\overline{\mathbb{CP}}^{2}
.

Here
H(I\pi)
denotes the hyperbolic form on
I\pi\oplus I\pi^{\dagger}
, where
I\pi:=\ker(\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\mathbb{Z})
is the augmentation ideal of the group ring and
I\pi^{\dagger}\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(I\pi,\mathbb{Z}\pi)
; see the conventions below.

Remark 1.13
.

Other known homeomorphism classifications of closed, oriented
4
-manifolds are due to Freedman–Quinn
[
16
]
for fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}
, Hambleton–Kreck
[
26
,
27
]
for finite cyclic fundamental groups, Hambleton–Kreck–Teichner
[
25
]
for some geometrically
2
-dimensional groups, especially solvable Baumslag–Solitar groups, and Hambleton–Hildum
[
22
]
for some cases (spin
+
and pre-stabilised) involving cohomological dimension 3 groups.

In the closed, nonorientable case we have classifications by Wang
[
70
]
for fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}
, Hambleton–Kreck–Teichner
[
24
]
for fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}/2
,

4
-manifolds homotopy equivalent to
\mathbb{RP}^{4}\#\mathbb{RP}^{4}
by Brookman–Davis–Khan
[
6
]
(see also Jahren–Kwasik
[
36
]
), and Hambleton–Hillman
[
23
]
for
4
-manifolds homotopy equivalent to quotients of
S^{2}\times S^{2}
.

1.3.
Homeomorphism classification for
3
-manifold fundamental groups

In
Sections

12.1
and
12.2
we place limitations on the
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules, and the sesquilinear Hermitian forms on them, that can arise as the invariants of
4
-manifolds.
In
Section

12.3
we consider what the surgery exact sequence tells us about the classification of homotopy equivalent
4
-manifolds whose fundamental group is a torsion-free
3
-manifold group.

Theorem

12.6
applies to all such groups, giving an upper bound on the number of
s
-cobordism classes of such
4
-manifolds, in both the smooth and topological categories.
Then specialising again to solvable groups we obtain the following result on the homeomorphism classification.

Corollary 1.14
.

Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold whose fundamental group
\pi
is a torsion-free, solvable
3
-manifold group.
There are at most two homeomorphism classes
(
including that of
M)
of closed
4
-manifolds with quadratic
2
-type isomorphic to
Q(M)

and with the same Kirby–Siebenmann invariant.

Conventions and notation

All manifolds are assumed to be closed, connected, and based. They are considered as topological manifolds by default. Our
n
-manifolds
X
are also assumed to be endowed with a local orientation at the basepoint, which determines a (twisted) fundamental class
[X]\in H_{n}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
.
Homotopy equivalences, homeomorphisms, and stable homeomorphisms are assumed to respect the basepoint and the local orientation, and hence in the oriented case to be orientation preserving.

We use the symbol
C_{2}
to denote the multiplicative group
\{\pm 1\}
. The symbol
\mathbb{Z}/2
denotes the additive group
\{0,1\}
. The symbol
\Sigma_{2}
denotes the symmetric group on two elements. The symbol
\mathbb{F}_{2}
denotes the field of two elements. The symbol
\mathbb{Z}^{-}
denotes the integers as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
-module, where
1\in\mathbb{Z}/2
acts by multiplication by
-1
.

We will always assume that
\pi
is a finitely presented group and that
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
is a homomorphism. When we say that a
4
-manifold
M
has fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w
, we mean that
M
comes with an identification
\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi
, such that
\pi_{1}(M)\to\pi\xrightarrow{w}C_{2}
is the orientation character of
M
.

For a topological space
X
homotopy equivalent to a CW complex, the Postnikov
2
-type is denoted by
P_{2}(X)
. We always choose a model CW complex for
P_{2}(X)
.

For a left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
A
we write
A^{\dagger}
for the left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module given by
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(A,\mathbb{Z}\pi)
, turned into a left module using the involution of
\mathbb{Z}\pi
sending
g\mapsto w(g)g^{-1}
. Similarly, for a map
f\colon A\to B
of
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules, we have the induced left module homomorphism
f^{\dagger}\colon B^{\dagger}\to A^{\dagger}
.
When
G\leq\pi
is a subgroup, for a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
A
, we will denote the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\mathbb{Z}G)
by
A^{\star}
.

Outline

Section

2
gives our promised criteria in
Theorem

2.4
. This can be combined with
[
26
,
3
]
to provide homotopy classifications. This combination is presented as
Corollary

2.8
, which states that homotopy classifications can be proven for
4
-manifolds satisfying conditions (
1
) – (
4
) of
Theorem

2.4
.

Sections

3
,
4
,
5
,
6
,
7
, and
8
are concerned with showing that these conditions are satisfied by the
4
-manifolds considered in
Theorem

1.4
. More precisely, in
Section

3
we prove general results about the second homotopy groups of
4
-manifolds and in
Section

4
we show that
Theorem

2.4
(
1
) holds in our setting. Next, in
Section

5
we consider the injectivity of the map
\ev^{*}
, which is condition (
4
) of
Theorem

2.4
.
In
Section

6
we give general criteria under which the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
is injective, where
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Condition (
3
) of
Theorem

2.4
calls for the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
to be contained in the kernel of the map
\varphi_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
. That this holds for the
4
-manifolds in
Theorem

1.4
is shown in
Section

7
.
Section

8
introduces a property of groups, that we call Property 4HL, short for
4
th Homology Lifting
. This property is useful to establish
Theorem

2.4
(
2
). We show that the groups considered in
Theorem

1.4
have Property 4HL.
With these results in hand, we give the proof of
Theorem

1.4
in
Section

9
.

In
Section

10
we present two more applications of
Corollary

2.8
, using it to recover
[
25
]
*Theorem 5.13 and
[
26
]
*Theorem 1.1(i).
Then in
Section

11
we prove
Theorem

1.10
.

In
Section

12
we provide additional information on various classifications of
4
-manifolds with torsion-free
3
-manifold fundamental groups.

2.
General statements on homotopy classification

In this section we state and prove
Theorem

2.4
, which will be our main technical tool going forward. Before doing so we recall the result of
[
26
,
3
]
and the definition of Whitehead’s universal quadratic functor. We end the section by giving a more detailed outline of the upcoming proof of
Theorem

1.4
.

2.1.
Classification via the fundamental triple

In general, the homotopy classification of closed
4
-manifolds is given by the
fundamental triple
: the Postnikov
2
-type
B
, the orientation character
w
, and the image of the fundamental class in
H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
. This was shown by Baues and Bleile
[
3
]
*Corollary 3.2, extending work of Hambleton and Kreck
[
26
]
*Theorem 1.1, in the following result.

Theorem 2.1

(
[
3
]
[
26
]
)
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed
4
-manifolds, both with fundamental group
\pi
. Let
B
be a connected,
3
-coconnected
CW
complex, also with fundamental group identified with
\pi
. Fix a homomorphism
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. Assume there are
3
-connected maps
f\colon M\to B
and
f^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to B
inducing the identity maps on fundamental groups, and such that
w\circ f_{*},w\circ f^{\prime}_{*}\colon\pi\to C_{2}
give the orientation characters of
M
and
M^{\prime}
respectively. If
f_{*}([M])=f^{\prime}_{*}([M^{\prime}])\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
, then
M\simeq M^{\prime}
, via a
(
basepoint and local orientation preserving
)
homotopy equivalence that induces the identity on fundamental groups and induces
(f^{\prime}_{*})^{-1}\circ f_{*}\colon\pi_{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{2}(M^{\prime})
.

The main difficulty in applying
Theorem

2.1
lies in computing the image of the fundamental class. In the case of finite fundamental groups, it is known in some cases that the image of the fundamental class in the homology of the Postnikov
2
-type is determined by the equivariant intersection form. More precisely, by work of Hambleton and Kreck
[
26
]
*Theorem 1.1 (i) together with an improvement by Teichner
[
67
]
(see
[
45
]
*Corollary 1.5 for the published version), for finite fundamental groups the quadratic
2
-type of
M
determines its homotopy type if the abelian group
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
is torsion-free.
Here
\Gamma
denotes Whitehead’s universal quadratic functor, which we recall presently. For
w=0
, this condition was shown to hold for finite groups that are cyclic
[
26
]
, abelian with 2 generators
[
42
]
and dihedral
[
39
]
. In
Section

2.3
we state and prove
Theorem

2.4
, which gives a generalisation of the Hambleton–Kreck criterion that can be applied to all fundamental groups.

2.2.
Whitehead’s
\Gamma
groups

In
[
73
]
, Whitehead defined the universal quadratic functor
\Gamma
.
Here, a function
f\colon A\to B
between abelian groups is said to be
quadratic
if
f(-a)=f(a)
for all
a\in A
and if the function
A\times A\to B
, given by
(a,b)\mapsto f(a+b)-f(a)-f(b)
is bilinear. The functor
\Gamma
is the universal quadratic functor in the sense that there exists a quadratic map
\gamma\colon A\to\Gamma(A)
with the property that for every quadratic map
f\colon A\to B
there exists a unique linear map
\Gamma(f)\colon\Gamma(A)\to B
with
f=\Gamma(f)\circ\gamma
.

For a free abelian group
A
, the group
\Gamma(A)
is isomorphic to the group of fixed points of
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A
under the
\Sigma_{2}
-action permuting the two copies of
A
.
If
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module then the diagonal action of
\pi
gives
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A

the structure of a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module, and
\Gamma(A)\subseteq A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A

is a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-submodule.
A direct consequence of
[
73
]
*Sections 10 and 13 is that for a connected,
3
-coconnected CW complex
B
with fundamental group
\pi
, we have an isomorphism
\Gamma(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))\cong H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
.

2.3.
A criterion for homotopy classification via the quadratic
2
-type

Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. We consider the equivariant intersection form
\lambda_{M}
as a Hermitian form on
H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
. For a left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
A
we denote the left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module given by
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(A,\mathbb{Z}\pi)
, turned into a left module using the involution of
\mathbb{Z}\pi
sending
g\mapsto w(g)g^{-1}
, by
A^{\dagger}
. We denote the group of Hermitian forms on a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
C
by
\Her^{w}(C)
and we denote sesquilinear forms by
\Sesq^{w}(C)
. When
w
is the constant map, we may suppress the superscript. When
A
is free as an abelian group, we have the homomorphism

(2.2)

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)

\displaystyle\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})

\displaystyle a\otimes b

\displaystyle\mapsto((f,g)\mapsto\overline{f(a)}g(b)),

as in
[
33
]
*Section 7.

Remark 2.3
.

This is a special case of the following, which defines
\mathcal{B}_{A}
also when
A
is not necessarily free as an abelian group.
Consider the quadratic function
f\colon A\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
given by
a\mapsto((f,g)\mapsto\overline{f(a)}g(a))
. By the universal property of
\Gamma
, there is an induced map
\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
, and it is straightforward to check that this factors through
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)
, yielding a homomorphism
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
. We omit the details, since henceforth we will only consider cases where
A
is free as an abelian group.

Recall from the previous section that by
[
73
]
*Sections 10 and 13, for a connected,
3
-coconnected CW complex
B
with fundamental group
\pi
, we have an isomorphism

\Upsilon\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)).

For any connected CW complex
B
with fundamental group
\pi
, let

\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))

denote the homomorphism induced by the evaluation map
\ev\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}
taking
\alpha\mapsto(x\mapsto\alpha\cap x)
. Let

\varphi_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})

denote the homomorphism given by reduction of coefficients.
Now we state our main technical theorem.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. Let
B
be a connected,
3
-coconnected
CW
complex with fundamental group also identified with
\pi
, and let
f\colon M\to B
and
f^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to B
be maps that induce the identity on fundamental groups. Assume further that, for some
k\in\mathbb{Z}
, the following holds.

(1)

The module
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group.

(2)

The difference
k(f_{*}[M]-(f^{\prime})_{*}[M^{\prime}])\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
lies in the image of the map
\varphi_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
.

(3)

The kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon
is contained in the kernel of
\varphi_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
.

(4)

The map
\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
is injective.

Then
kf_{*}[M]=kf^{\prime}_{*}[M^{\prime}]\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
if and only if
f_{*}\lambda_{M}=f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}}\in\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
.

Remark 2.5
.

We will only apply
Theorem

2.4
(in
Sections

10
and
9
) in the case
k=1
. We prove the result for general
k
in case this version is useful in the future.

Remark 2.6
.

We will see in the upcoming proof of
Theorem

2.4
that rather than conditions (
3
) and (
4
) above, we only need the kernel of the composition
\ev^{*}\circ\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon
to be contained in the kernel of
\varphi_{B}
. We prefer the current formulation since in our applications of this result
we will verify the conditions individually. As we will show in
Section

5
, there are several general methods to conclude the injectivity of
\ev^{*}
, and in the setting of
Theorem

1.4
the map
\ev^{*}
is in fact an isomorphism.

Proof of
Theorem

2.4
.

Consider the diagram

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{B}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev^{*}}

Here we used (
1
) to define the map
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
.
The map
\Theta_{B}
is defined using the cap product as

x\mapsto\big((\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\langle\beta,\alpha\cap x\rangle\big).

Commutativity of the diagram follows as in the proof of
[
33
]
*Lemma 10. Since
\lambda_{M}
and
\lambda_{M^{\prime}}
are induced by the cap product with the corresponding fundamental class, it follows from naturality of the cap product that
k(f_{*}[M]-f^{\prime}_{*}[M^{\prime}])\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
maps to
k(f_{*}\lambda_{M}-f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}})\in\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
under
\Theta_{B}
.

The only if direction can now be proven without using (
2
), (
3
), or (
4
). Suppose that

kf_{*}[M]=kf^{\prime}_{*}[M^{\prime}]\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w}).

Then
k(f_{*}[M]-f^{\prime}_{*}[M^{\prime}])=0
and hence
k(f_{*}\lambda_{M}-f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}})=0\in\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
.
However,
\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
is torsion-free since
\mathbb{Z}\pi
is torsion-free, and so

f_{*}\lambda_{M}=f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}}\in\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)).

To prove the if direction, we suppose that
f_{*}\lambda_{M}=f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}}\in\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
.
By (
2
), there is some
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
such that

\varphi_{B}(x)=k(f_{*}[M]-(f^{\prime})_{*}[M^{\prime}])\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w}).

Then

\Theta_{B}\circ\varphi_{B}(x)=\Theta_{B}(k(f_{*}[M]-(f^{\prime})_{*}[M^{\prime}]))=k(f_{*}\lambda_{M}-f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}})=0,

and so
by commutativity

\ev^{*}\circ\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon(x)=0.

By (
4
)
\ev^{*}
is injective, so
x\in\ker(\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon)
. By (
3
),
\ker(\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon)\subseteq\ker(\varphi_{B})
, and thus
k(f_{*}[M]-(f^{\prime})_{*}[M^{\prime}])=\varphi_{B}(x)=0
. It follows that
kf_{*}[M]=kf^{\prime}_{*}[M^{\prime}]\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
as desired.
∎

We will frequently apply the combination of
Theorems

2.1
and
2.4
to obtain our homotopy classifications. To make this easier, we give the statement that we will use in the following corollary.

Corollary 2.8
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. Let
B
be a connected,
3
-coconnected
CW
complex with fundamental group also identified with
\pi
. Let
f\colon M\to B
and
f^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to B
be
3
-connected maps inducing the identity on fundamental groups. Assume that, for
k=1
, the conditions (
1
) – (
4
) of
Theorem

2.4
hold. Let
g_{i}:=(f^{\prime})_{*}^{-1}\circ f_{*}\colon\pi_{i}(M)\to\pi_{i}(M^{\prime})
, and suppose that
(g_{1},g_{2})
induces an isomorphism between the quadratic
2
-type of
M
and that of
M^{\prime}
. Then there exists a
(
basepoint and local orientation preserving
)
homotopy equivalence
h\colon M\xrightarrow{\simeq}M^{\prime}
such that
h_{*}=g_{i}\colon\pi_{i}(M)\to\pi_{i}(M^{\prime})
for
i=1,2
.

Proof.

By assumption, conditions (
1
) – (
4
) of
Theorem

2.4
hold, so we can apply that theorem, for
k=1
. The assumption that
(g_{1},g_{2})
induces an isomorphism between the quadratic
2
-type of
M
and that of
M^{\prime}
implies that
f_{*}\lambda_{M}=f^{\prime}_{*}\lambda_{M^{\prime}}\in\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
. Hence
Theorem

2.4
implies that
f_{*}[M]=f^{\prime}_{*}[M^{\prime}]\in H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
. Now
Theorem

2.1
implies that the desired homotopy equivalence
h\colon M\to M^{\prime}
exists.
∎

2.4.
Outline of the proof of
Theorem

1.4

We will use
Corollary

2.8
to prove
Theorem

1.4
, with the culmination of the proof presented in
Section

9
. That is, we will show that conditions (
1
) – (
4
) of
Theorem

2.4
hold for closed
4
-manifolds with
3
-manifold fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w
, such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
We sketch the proof for each condition individually next.

(1)

In
Section

4
we introduce a property of groups which we call
P2FA
(
Definition

4.1
). By definition if a
4
-manifold
M
has P2FA fundamental group, then
\pi_{2}(M)
is free as an abelian group. We will show in
Proposition

4.10
that
3
-manifold groups are P2FA, which shows that
Theorem

2.4
(
1
) holds in our setting.

(2)

In
Section

8
we introduce the
4
th homology lifting property
of a pair
(\pi,w)
, which we abbreviate to Property 4HL, where
\pi
is a group and
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
is a homomorphism. Roughly, this property gives a criterion to decide whether an element in the codomain of
\varphi_{X}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\to H_{4}(X;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
lies in the image of
\varphi_{X}
, for an arbitrary CW complex
X
with
\pi_{1}(X)=\pi
and homomorphism
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. This is helpful for proving
Theorem

2.4
(
2
) since we can apply the criterion to the element
f_{*}[M]-(f^{\prime})_{*}[M^{\prime}]
, using the notation of
Theorem

2.4
. We will show in
Proposition

8.22
that
(\pi,w)
has Property 4HL when
\pi
is as in
Theorem

1.4
, enabling the application of the criterion in our setting. For the proof we first address the cases of finite groups,
PD_{3}
-groups, the infinite cyclic group, and the group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
individually. In these cases the primary tool is the Leray–Serre spectral sequence for the fibration
\widetilde{B}\to B\to BH
, where
B
is a connected, 2-coconnected CW complex and
H=\pi_{1}(B)
. With these individual cases established, the final result is proven by considering how Property 4HL behaves under free products.

(3)

In
Section

6
we give general criteria under which the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
is injective, where
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module.
When
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
is injective, for
B
and
\pi
as in
Theorem

2.4
, since
\Upsilon
is an isomorphism it follows that condition (
3
) is satisfied. However, in our setting, the map
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
is not necessarily injective. Therefore in
Section

7
we specialise to the case of admissible
3
-manifold groups and orientation characters. Here we use our previous results on the second homotopy groups from
Section

3
and once again use the Leray–Serre spectral sequence. Special care is needed for the case of
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
fundamental group. Finally we show in
Corollary

7.10
that
Theorem

2.4
(
3
) holds in our setting.

(4)

We will show in
Proposition

5.9
that for
4
-manifolds with
3
-manifold fundamental group the map
\ev^{*}
is in fact an isomorphism, showing that
Theorem

2.4
(
4
) holds. The proof of
Proposition

5.9
is rather general, depending on the homology and cohomology groups of
\pi
with
\mathbb{Z}\pi
coefficients, and consisting of an analysis of certain exact sequences arising from the universal coefficient spectral sequence.

We end this section by remarking that while
Sections

4
and
7
are rather specific to our setting,
Sections

3
,
5
,
6
, and
8
contain a number of general results that are likely to be useful to those interested in proving homotopy classification results for other fundamental groups.

3.
The second homotopy group
for
4
-manifolds with
3
-manifold fundamental group

In this section we study
\pi_{2}(M)\cong H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
for
4
-manifolds
M
whose fundamental group
\pi
is a
3
-manifold group.
In
Section

3.1
we recall a general result on the stable isomorphism class of
\pi_{2}(M)
, without restriction on the fundamental group.
In
Section

3.2
we consider cases for which
\pi_{1}(M)
is the fundamental group of an irreducible
3
-manifold.
In
Section

3.3
, we investigate
\pi_{2}(M)
when
\pi_{1}(M)
is a
3
-manifold group that is an admissible nontrivial free product.
Finally in
Section

3.4
we prove that the stable isomorphism class of
\pi_{2}(M)
determines the image of the fundamental class of
M
in
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
, where
\pi=\pi_{1}(M)
.

We will express the stable isomorphism classes of the second homotopy groups in terms of twisted augmentation ideals, which we define next.

Definition 3.1
.

Let
v\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism. Let
I\pi^{v}\unlhd\mathbb{Z}\pi
denote the
twisted augmentation ideal
, i.e. the kernel of the twisted augmentation map

\varepsilon_{v}\colon\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\mathbb{Z}^{v}

sending
g\in\pi
to
v(g)
. When
v
is the constant map to
1\in C_{2}
, we use the symbol
I\pi
for the (untwisted) augmentation ideal.

We will use the following elementary lemma several times, so we record it here.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\pi
be a group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism.
The map

(3.3)

\displaystyle\omega\colon\mathbb{Z}\pi

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{Z}\pi;\;\;\;\sum_{g\in\pi}n_{g}g\mapsto\sum_{g\in\pi}w(g)n_{g}g.

induces a left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module isomorphism
\omega\colon\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}\pi
.

3.1.
A general result on
\pi_{2}(M)

We start this section by recording the following fact regarding the stable isomorphism types of second homotopy groups of
4
-manifolds. Recall that two
4
-manifolds are said to be
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stably homeomorphic
if they become homeomorphic after connected sum with copies of
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
and
\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
, along with classifying maps
c_{M}\colon M\to B\pi
and
c_{M^{\prime}}\colon M^{\prime}\to B\pi

(
not necessarily inducing the identity on
\pi_{1})
. If
(c_{M})_{*}([M])=(c_{M^{\prime}})_{*}([M^{\prime}])\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})/\pm\Aut(\pi)
, then
\pi_{2}(M)
and
\pi_{2}(M^{\prime})
are stably isomorphic as
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules.

Proof.

By
[
51
]
(see also
[
44
]
*Theorem 1.2, Section 1.5), in the case that
(c_{M})_{*}[M]=(c_{M^{\prime}})_{*}[M^{\prime}]\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})/\pm\Aut(\pi)
, the manifolds
M
and
M^{\prime}
are
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stably homeomorphic if and only if they have equal Kirby–Siebenmann invariants. In other words, possibly after connected sum with a copy of
\star\mathbb{CP}^{2}
, they become
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
-stably homeomorphic.
Connected sum with
\mathbb{CP}^{2}
,
\overline{\mathbb{CP}^{2}}
, or
\star\mathbb{CP}^{2}
changes the second homotopy group by direct sum with
\mathbb{Z}\pi
. This completes the proof.
∎

3.2.
Irreducible
3
-manifold groups

In this section we study
\pi_{2}(M)
for
4
-manifolds
M
such that
\pi_{1}(M)
is
either
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, infinite cyclic, a finite
3
-manifold group, or a
PD_{3}
-group. We will consider these cases individually.

In the following special case of
[
26
]
*Proposition 2.4
[
28
]
*Theorem 4.2
[
44
]
*Proposition 1.10,
we use the fact that
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})=0
for the group
\pi
in the statement. When
\pi
is a torsion-free
3
-manifold group this follows from the fact that the cohomological dimension
\operatorname{cd}\pi\leq 3
.

Lemma 3.5

(
[
26
,
28
,
44
]
)
.

Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold such that
\pi=\pi_{1}(M)
is cyclic or a torsion-free
3
-manifold group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be the orientation character of
M
. Assume that
w
is trivial if
\pi
is finite cyclic. Let
F\xrightarrow{d_{2}}G\twoheadrightarrow I\pi
be a presentation for the augmentation ideal
I\pi
and write
d_{2}^{\dagger}\colon G^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{}F^{\dagger}
for the dual map, where
F
and
G
are free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules. Recall that here we use the involution
g\mapsto w(g)g^{-1}
to turn
G^{\dagger}
and
F^{\dagger}
into left modules.
Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
\ker(d_{2})\oplus\coker(d_{2}^{\dagger})
.

Recall that an
R
-module
A
is said to be
stably free
if there exist
m,n\geq 0
such that
A\oplus R^{n}\cong R^{m}
.

Lemma 3.6
.

Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with infinite cyclic fundamental group. Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is free.

Proof.

The augmentation ideal
I\mathbb{Z}
is free as a
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]
-module. Then
0\to I\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{Id}}I\mathbb{Z}
is a presentation for
I\mathbb{Z}
, and hence
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably free by
Lemma

3.5
. For every
k
, any projective
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}^{k}]
-module is free
[
65
]
*Theorem 1.1, see also
[
53
]
*Corollary V.4.12. Hence
\pi_{2}(M)
is free as claimed.
∎

For
4
-manifolds with fundamental group a finite
3
-manifold group, we have the following special case of
[
26
]
*Remark 2.5. The application requires the fact that an arbitrary finite
3
-manifold group has 4-periodic cohomology and that for finite
3
-manifold groups the projective modules
P_{i}
in
[
26
]
*Remark 2.5 can be chosen to be free.

Lemma 3.7

(
[
26
]
)
.

Let
\pi
be a finite
3
-manifold group. Let
M
be a closed, orientable
4
-manifold with
fundamental group
\pi
.
Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(I\pi,\mathbb{Z})
.

In the special case of an orientable
4
-manifold with finite cyclic fundamental group
\pi
, we deduce that
\pi_{2}(M)
is isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus I\pi
, as we see next.

Lemma 3.8
.

For the group
\pi:=\mathbb{Z}/n
, the dual
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(I\pi,\mathbb{Z})=I\pi^{*}
of the augmentation ideal
I\pi
is isomorphic to
I\pi
. In particular, for a closed, orientable
4
-manifold
M
with fundamental group
\pi
, the second homotopy group
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus I\pi
.

Proof.

Let
\pi:=\mathbb{Z}/n=\langle T\mid T^{n}\rangle
. Dualise the sequence
0\to I\pi\to\mathbb{Z}\pi\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathbb{Z}\to 0
, where
\varepsilon\colon\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\mathbb{Z}
is the augmentation map, to obtain

(\mathbb{Z}\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z}))\xrightarrow{N}(\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z}\pi,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(I\pi,\mathbb{Z})\to\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{Z})=0.

It follows that the cokernel of the norm map
\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{N}\mathbb{Z}\pi
sending
1\mapsto 1+T+\cdots+T^{n-1}
is isomorphic to
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(I\pi,\mathbb{Z})
.
From the short exact sequence

0\to\mathbb{Z}\xrightarrow{N}\mathbb{Z}\pi\xrightarrow{1-T}\mathbb{Z}\pi\xrightarrow{\varepsilon}\mathbb{Z},

we see that
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}}(I\pi,\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\pi/\operatorname{Im}N\cong\mathbb{Z}\pi/\ker(1-T)\cong\operatorname{Im}(1-T)\cong\ker\varepsilon=I\pi
, as needed. The second statement now follows from
Lemma

3.7
since
\mathbb{Z}/n
is a finite
3
-manifold group.
∎

The analogues of the previous lemmas do not hold for nonorientable
4
-manifolds as can be seen by considering
\mathbb{RP}^{4}
.
This is a further reason that we restrict ourselves in
Theorem

1.4
to cases where the orientation character is trivial on the finite cyclic subgroups.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\pi=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2=\langle t,T\mid[T,t],T^{2}\rangle
and let
v^{\prime}\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be given by
v^{\prime}(t)=1
and
v^{\prime}(T)=-1
. Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w
such that
w(T)=1
. Let
v:=wv^{\prime}
. Let
c\colon M\to B\pi
induce an isomorphism on fundamental groups. Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably free if
c_{*}([M])\neq 0
in
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
and otherwise
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v}
if
c_{*}([M])=0
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.4
, for a fixed
w
, the stable isomorphism class of
\pi_{2}(M)
only depends on
c_{*}[M]
.

By
[
43
]
*Lemma 7.5, the module
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably free if
c_{*}[M]\neq 0
and
w
is trivial. For
w(t)=-1
, the same holds, because we can construct a model with trivial
\pi_{2}
, as follows.
Let
\tau\colon\mathbb{RP}^{3}\to\mathbb{RP}^{3}
be an orientation reversing self-homeomorphism, e.g. induced by reflection across the equator of
S^{3}
.
Then the mapping torus
T_{\tau}
of
\tau
has orientation character
w
and
c_{*}[T_{\tau}]\neq 0
. Also it has trivial
\pi_{2}
, by the long exact sequence of the fibration
\mathbb{RP}^{3}\to T_{\tau}\to S^{1}
. It follows that
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably free for all
4
-manifolds with orientation character
w
and
c_{*}[M]\neq 0
, as needed.

In
[
44
]
*Section 5, it was shown that if
c_{*}[M]=0
, then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
\ker d_{2}\oplus\coker d^{2}_{w}
, where
(C_{*},d_{*})
is the standard 2-periodic free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces t-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-t}

and
d^{2}_{w}
denotes the cochain map from the cochain complex
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(C_{*},\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w})
.

The summand
\ker d_{2}
is independent of
w
, and as in
[
43
]
*Lemma 7.11 we have

\ker d_{2}\cong\operatorname{Im}d_{3}\cong C_{3}/\ker d_{3}\cong C_{3}/\operatorname{Im}d_{4}\cong C_{1}/\operatorname{Im}d_{2}\cong C_{1}/\ker d_{1}\cong\operatorname{Im}d_{1}\cong I\pi.

The map
d^{2}_{w}
is given by

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-w(t)t}

We show that the cokernel of this map is
I\pi^{v}
.
Recall the
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module isomorphism

\mathbb{Z}\pi^{v}\xrightarrow{\cong}\mathbb{Z}\pi
from
Lemma

3.2
.
Tensoring the above free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
with
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{v}
over
\mathbb{Z}\pi
and applying this isomorphism, we obtain the free resolution

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{v}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\oplus}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{2}^{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+T}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{1}^{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varepsilon_{v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-w(t)t}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1+T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces w(t)t-1}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-w(t)t}

Thus we have

\coker d^{2}_{w}\cong\coker d_{2}^{v}\cong C_{1}/\operatorname{Im}d_{2}^{v}\cong C_{1}/\ker d_{1}^{v}\cong\operatorname{Im}d_{1}^{v}\cong\ker\varepsilon_{v}=:I\pi^{v}.

So
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v}
, as required.
∎

Finally we consider the case of
PD_{3}
-groups. Below note that since any two aspherical
PD_{3}
-complexes with the same fundamental group are homotopy equivalent, the orientation character for a
PD_{3}
group is well-defined. Also note that in this case the orientation character is determined by
\pi
, via the natural right action of
\pi
on
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\mathbb{Z}
. Hence
v^{\prime}
in the next lemma is defined in terms of
\pi
.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
\pi
be a
PD_{3}
-group. Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. Let
v^{\prime}
be the orientation character of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex associated to
\pi
.
Let
v:=wv^{\prime}
.
Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi^{v}
.

Proof.

Let
X
be an aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
\pi
. We can assume that
X
has a single 0- and
3
-cell. Consider the cellular
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-chain complex

C_{3}(\widetilde{X})\xrightarrow{d_{3}}C_{2}(\widetilde{X})\xrightarrow{d_{2}}C_{1}(\widetilde{X})\xrightarrow{d_{1}}C_{0}(\widetilde{X})

of
X
. Since
X
is aspherical,
C_{1}/\operatorname{Im}d_{2}\cong C_{1}/\ker d_{1}\cong\operatorname{Im}d_{1}=\ker\varepsilon=I\pi
, and so
d_{2}
is a presentation homomorphism of
I\pi
. Moreover
\ker d_{2}=C_{3}(\widetilde{X})
is free. By
Lemma

3.5
,
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
\coker d_{2}^{\dagger}
. Since
X
is a
PD_{3}
-complex with orientation character
v^{\prime}
,
d_{2}^{\dagger}
is a presentation homomorphism for
I\pi^{v}
by Poincaré duality. It follows that
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi^{v}
.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

Let
\pi
be a
PD_{3}
-group. Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
.
Then
\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
is finitely generated and stably free.

Proof.

First note that
\pi_{2}(M)
is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be the orientation character of
M
and let
v^{\prime}
be the orientation character of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex associated to
\pi
. By
Lemma

3.10
,
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to the twisted augmentation ideal
I\pi^{v}
, where
v:=wv^{\prime}
. Hence it suffices to show that
(I\pi^{v})^{\dagger}
is finitely generated and free. Dualising the short exact sequence
I\pi^{v}\to\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\mathbb{Z}^{v}
, we obtain the exact sequence

(\mathbb{Z}^{v})^{\dagger}\to(\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\to(I\pi^{v})^{\dagger}\to\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\mathbb{Z}^{v},\mathbb{Z}\pi).

Since
\pi
is infinite,
(\mathbb{Z}^{v})^{\dagger}=0
. Furthermore we have

\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\mathbb{Z}^{v},\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong H^{1}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi^{v})\cong H_{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi^{vv^{\prime}})=0.

Here we used that
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{vv^{\prime}}=\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w}
is a free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module, because it is isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\pi
via the map
\omega
from (
3.3
).
Hence
(I\pi^{v})^{\dagger}\cong(\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}
is finitely generated and free, as required.
∎

3.3.
3
-manifold groups that are free products

Now we start considering
\pi_{2}(M)
in the case that
\pi_{1}(M)
is a
3
-manifold group that is a nontrivial free product. The first few results hold generally, but we quickly specialise to the case that the pair
(\pi_{1}(M),w_{M})
is admissible, as in
Theorem

1.4
.

Lemma 3.12
.

Let
G
and
H
be finitely presented groups. Let
w\colon G\to C_{2}
and
w^{\prime}\colon H\to C_{2}
be homomorphisms.
Then the twisted augmentation ideal
I(G*H)^{w*w^{\prime}}
of
G*H
is isomorphic to the direct sum
\Ind_{G}^{G*H}IG^{w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{G*H}IH^{w^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

Choose a model for
BG
and tensor its
\mathbb{Z}G
-chain complex with
\mathbb{Z}G^{w}
over
\mathbb{Z}G
to obtain a free resolution

\cdots\to C_{2}^{G,w}\xrightarrow{d_{2}^{G,w}}C_{1}^{G,w}\xrightarrow{d_{1}^{G,w}}\mathbb{Z}G^{w}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{G,w}}\mathbb{Z}^{w}.

Similarly, a model for
BH
gives a free resolution

\cdots\to C_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{d_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}}}C_{1}^{H,w^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{d_{1}^{H,w^{\prime}}}\mathbb{Z}H\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{H,w^{\prime}}}\mathbb{Z}^{w^{\prime}}.

Here the module homomorphism
\varepsilon^{G,w}\colon\mathbb{Z}G^{w}\to\mathbb{Z}^{w}
is the unique left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module homomorphism sending
1\mapsto 1
, which sends
\sum n_{g}g\mapsto\sum n_{g}
.
It factors as

\varepsilon^{G,w}\colon\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w}\xrightarrow{\omega,\cong}\mathbb{Z}\pi\xrightarrow{\varepsilon_{w}}\mathbb{Z}^{w},

using the left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module isomorphism
\omega
from
Lemma

3.2
and the map
\varepsilon_{w}
from
Definition

3.1
.
It follows that
\omega
induces a left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module isomorphism
\ker\varepsilon^{G,w}\cong\ker\varepsilon_{w}=IG^{w}
.
So by exactness
IG^{w}\cong\ker\varepsilon^{G,w}\cong\coker(d_{2}^{G,w})
and
IH^{w^{\prime}}\cong\ker\varepsilon^{H,w^{\prime}}\cong\coker(d_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}})
.

Write
\pi:=G*H
.
The space
BG\vee BH
is a model for
B(G*H)
. So we can take its
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-chain complex and tensor with
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w*w^{\prime}}
over
\mathbb{Z}\pi
(which does not affect exactness) to obtain a free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}^{w*w^{\prime}}
. To compare it with the previous resolutions for
G
and
H
, we need the following.

For every
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
A
, there is an isomorphism
\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(A)^{w*w^{\prime}}\cong\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(A^{w})
given by
\gamma\otimes a\mapsto(w*w^{\prime})(\gamma)\gamma\otimes a
for all
\gamma\in\pi
and
a\in A
. Similarly,
\Ind_{H}^{\pi}(A^{\prime})^{w*w^{\prime}}\cong\Ind_{H}^{\pi}((A^{\prime})^{w^{\prime}})
for every
\mathbb{Z}H
-module
A^{\prime}
.
To use this consider

C_{*}(B\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong C_{*}(BG\vee BH;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\Ind_{G}^{\pi}C_{*}^{G}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}C_{*}^{H}.

Tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w*w^{\prime}}
over
\mathbb{Z}\pi
yields

(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}C_{i}^{G})^{w*w^{\prime}}\oplus(\Ind_{H}^{\pi}C_{i}^{H})^{w*w^{\prime}}\cong\Ind_{G}^{\pi}C_{i}^{G,w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}C_{i}^{H,w^{\prime}}.

Hence the
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-chain complex of
BG\vee BH
tensored with
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w*w^{\prime}}
over
\mathbb{Z}\pi
gives the free resolution

\displaystyle\cdots\to\Ind_{G}^{\pi}C_{2}^{G,w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}C_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\Ind_{G}^{\pi}d_{2}^{G,w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}d_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}}}\Ind_{G}^{\pi}C_{1}^{G,w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}C_{1}^{H,w^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\Ind_{G}^{\pi}d_{1}^{G,w}+\Ind_{H}^{\pi}d_{1}^{H,w^{\prime}}}\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w*w^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{\pi,w*w^{\prime}}}\mathbb{Z}^{w*w^{\prime}}.

Hence by exactness

\displaystyle I(G*H)^{w*w^{\prime}}\cong I\pi^{w*w^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\cong\coker(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}d_{2}^{G,w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}d_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}})\cong\Ind_{G}^{\pi}\coker(d_{2}^{G,w})\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}\coker(d_{2}^{H,w^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\cong\Ind_{G}^{\pi}IG^{w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}IH^{w^{\prime}}\cong\Ind_{G}^{G*H}IG^{w}\oplus\Ind_{H}^{G*H}IH^{w^{\prime}}

as desired.
∎

We will use the following useful fact several times, which is due to the first-named author
[
31
]
; an alternative proof was given later in
[
50
]
. When
Lemma

3.13
applies we say that
M

stably splits as a connected sum
.

Lemma 3.13

(
[
31
]
)
.

Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold and suppose that
\pi_{1}(M)\cong*_{i=1}^{n}G_{i}
. Then up to connected sum with copies of
S^{2}\times S^{2}
,
M
is homeomorphic to a connected sum of
4
-manifolds
\#_{i=1}^{n}M_{i}
with fundamental groups
\pi_{1}(M_{i})\cong G_{i}
.

The previous lemma is helpful since for a connected sum
M_{1}\#M_{2}
of manifolds
M
and
N
, we have that

(3.14)

\pi_{2}(M_{1}\#M_{2})\cong\Ind_{\pi_{1}(M_{1})}^{\pi_{1}(M_{1})*\pi_{1}(M_{2})}\pi_{2}(M_{1})\oplus\Ind_{\pi_{1}(M_{2})}^{\pi_{1}(M_{1})*\pi_{1}(M_{2})}\pi_{2}(M_{2}).

Therefore, for a
4
-manifold
M
with
\pi_{1}(M)=\pi
, connected sum with
S^{2}\times S^{2}
adds copies of
\mathbb{Z}\pi\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi
to the second homotopy group, so the stable isomorphism type does not change.

For the remainder of this section, let
(\pi,w)
be admissible, with
\pi
a
3
-manifold group and
w
an orientation character. Then, by the prime decomposition theorem for
3
-manifolds, there is a decomposition of the form

(3.15)

\pi\cong F*\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t}H_{k}\big),

for some
r,s,t\geq 0
, with
F
a free group,
Z_{i}
a finite cyclic group for each
i
,
G_{j}
a
PD_{3}
-group for each
j
and
H_{k}\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
for each
k
.

In the following proposition, and elsewhere, we will use that for either admissible
w\colon\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\cong H\to C_{2}
, we have
H_{4}(H;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
. When
w
is trivial, this is straightforward to see from the Künneth theorem. When
w
is nontrivial, this can be obtained by writing out the standard
\mathbb{Z}H
-module resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
, tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}^{w}
, and computing the homology.

Proposition 3.16
.

Fix a decomposition for
\pi
as in (
3.15
).
Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
By reordering the factors of
\pi
if needed, we assume that there exists
0\leq t^{\prime}\leq t
such that the image of the fundamental class
[M]
in

H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t}H_{4}(H_{k};\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{t}

is trivial in the first
t^{\prime}
summands and nontrivial for
k>t^{\prime}
.
Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to

\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime},

where

(3.17)

\Gamma=\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{k}\big)

and

(3.18)

\Gamma^{\prime}=\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{k}\big)

are subgroups of
\pi
in the canonical way. Here
v=wv^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to C_{2}
, where
v^{\prime}
is trivial on each
Z_{i}
, is the projection onto the second factor on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2

(
followed by the canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\to C_{2})
, and on each
G_{j}
factor it is the orientation character
u_{j}
of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
G_{j}
.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.13
,
M
is stably homeomorphic to

M^{F}\#\Big(\bighash_{i=1}^{r}M^{Z_{i}}\Big)\#\Big(\bighash_{j=1}^{s}M^{G_{j}}\Big)\#\Big(\bighash_{k=1}^{t}M^{H_{k}}\Big),

where each of
M^{F}
,
M^{Z_{i}}
,
M^{G_{j}}
, and
M^{H_{k}}
has fundamental group
F
,
Z_{i}
,
G_{j}
, and
H_{k}
, respectively.
Therefore, we know that
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to

(3.19)

\Ind_{F}^{\pi}\pi_{2}\big(M^{F}\big)\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Ind_{Z_{i}}^{\pi}\pi_{2}\big(M^{Z_{i}}\big)\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}\pi_{2}\big(M^{G_{j}}\big)\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t}\Ind_{H_{k}}^{\pi}\pi_{2}\big(M^{H_{k}}\big).

Next we will consider each summand individually.

By
Lemmas

3.6
and
3.13
, and using (
3.14
), we see that
\pi_{2}(M^{F})
is stably free. The induction is also stably free, so up to stable isomorphism, the first summand can be ignored.

By
Lemma

3.8
we know that each
\pi_{2}(M^{Z_{i}})
is stably isomorphic to
IZ_{i}\oplus IZ_{i}
. Similarly, by
Lemma

3.10
, each
\pi_{2}(M^{G_{j}})
is stably isomorphic to
IG_{j}^{v_{j}}
, where
v_{j}=wu_{j}\colon G_{j}\to C_{2}
and
u_{j}
is the orientation character of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
G_{j}
. The
M^{H_{k}}
factors are slightly more complicated. We saw in
Lemma

3.9
that
\pi_{2}(M^{H_{k}})
is isomorphic to
IH_{k}\oplus IH_{k}^{u}
, with
u=wu^{\prime}
and
u^{\prime}
the projection onto the second factor of
H_{k}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, followed by the isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{2}
, in the case of
k\leq t^{\prime}
, or
\pi_{2}(M^{H_{k}})
is stably free for
t\geq k>t^{\prime}
. Applying these facts to (
3.19
), we see that
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to

(3.20)

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Ind_{Z_{i}}^{\pi}(IZ_{i}\oplus IZ_{i})\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}IG_{j}^{v_{j}}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}\Ind_{H_{k}}^{\pi}(IH_{k}\oplus IH_{k}^{u})

\displaystyle\cong

\displaystyle\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Ind_{Z_{i}}^{\pi}IZ_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Ind_{Z_{i}}^{\pi}IZ_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}IG_{j}^{v_{j}}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}\Ind_{H_{k}}^{\pi}IH_{k}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}\Ind_{H_{k}}^{\pi}IH_{k}^{u}.

Here we use that the induction of a direct sum is the direct sum of the inductions.
We claim that this decomposition equals
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
. To see this,
start with each of
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}
and
\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
, and
apply
Lemma

3.12
iteratively, splitting off one factor at a time in the free product (
3.17
) or (
3.18
) respectively. Use that
\Ind_{K}^{\pi}\Ind_{H}^{K}=\Ind_{H}^{\pi}
for subgroups
H\leq K\leq\pi
.
This shows that

\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Ind_{Z_{i}}^{\pi}IZ_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{j=1}^{s}\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}IG_{j}^{v_{j}}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}\Ind_{H_{k}}^{\pi}IH_{k}^{u},

by definition of
v
. Similarly, we see that

\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}\Ind_{Z_{i}}^{\pi}IZ_{i}\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}\Ind_{H_{k}}^{\pi}IH_{k}.

To complete the proof note that each summand of (
3.20
) appears precisely once as a summand of precisely one of
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}
or
\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
.
∎

Corollary 3.21
.

Let
\pi
be a torsion-free
3
-manifold group. Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
Then
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi^{v}
where
v
is defined as follows. Since
\pi
is torsion-free, there is a decomposition
\pi\cong F\ast(\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j})
, where
F
is a free group and each
G_{j}
is a
PD_{3}
-group. Then we have
v=wv^{\prime}\colon\pi\to C_{2}
, where
v^{\prime}
is trivial on
F
, and on each
G_{j}
factor it is the orientation character
u_{j}
of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
G_{j}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.16
,
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}
, where
\Gamma=\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}
. Since
IF\cong\mathbb{Z}F
,
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi^{v}
by
Lemma

3.12
.
∎

3.4.
The second homotopy group
\pi_{2}(M)
determines the image of the fundamental class in
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})

We conclude the section with a result showing that the image
c_{*}([M])\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
of the fundamental class in the group homology is determined by the stable isomorphism class of
\pi_{2}(M)
, for a map
c\colon M\to B\pi
inducing an isomorphism on fundamental groups. We obtain a corollary (
Corollary

3.23
) that we will use in the proof of
Theorem

1.4
in
Section

9
in order to be able to apply Property 4HL; see
Section

8
for details on the latter.

Proposition 3.22
.

Fix a decomposition for
\pi
as in (
3.15
).
Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
Let
c\colon M\to B\pi
be a continuous map inducing the identity on fundamental groups.
By reordering the factors of
\pi
if needed, we assume that there exists
t^{\prime}\leq t
such that
c_{*}([M])
in

H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t}H_{4}(H_{k};\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{t}
is trivial in the first
t^{\prime}
summands and nontrivial for
k>t^{\prime}
.

Then
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably free as a
\mathbb{Z}H_{k}
-module if and only if
k>t^{\prime}
.
Here the
\pi
-action on
\mathbb{Z}H_{k}
is given by the projection to
H_{k}
and left multiplication.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.16
we know that
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to

\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime},

where

\Gamma=\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{k}\big)

and

\Gamma^{\prime}=\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{k}\big)

are subgroups of
\pi
in the canonical way. Here
v=wv^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to C_{2}
, where
v^{\prime}
is trivial on each
Z_{i}
, is the projection onto the second factor on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
followed by an isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{\cong}C_{2}
, and on the
G_{j}
it is the orientation character of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
G_{j}
. Then, for each
k
, the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably isomorphic to

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})\oplus\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}H_{k}).

Let us consider the two summands separately. We know by iteratively applying
Lemma

3.12
that

\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\cong\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\text{ a factor}\\
\text{ of }\Gamma\text{ in }\eqref{eqn:Gamma-splitting-big}\end{subarray}}\Ind_{L}^{\pi}IL^{v}.

Then, by adjunction, for each
k
the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}L\text{ a factor}\\
\text{ of }\Gamma\end{subarray}}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}L}(IL^{v},\Res_{L}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}H_{k}).

Similarly, using
Lemma

3.12
and adjunction,
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is isomorphic to

\bigoplus_{\begin{subarray}{c}L^{\prime}\text{ a factor}\\
\text{ of }\Gamma^{\prime}\end{subarray}}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}L^{\prime}}(IL^{\prime},\Res_{L^{\prime}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}H_{k}).

By hypothesis the
\pi
action on
\mathbb{Z}H_{k}
is given by projection to
H_{k}
. Therefore,

\Res_{L}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}H_{k}=\begin{cases}\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}H_{k},&\text{if }L\neq H_{k}\\
\mathbb{Z}H_{k},&\text{if }L=H_{k}.\end{cases}

Here we write
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}H_{k}
to emphasise that the
L
action on
\mathbb{Z}H_{k}
is trivial when
L\neq H_{k}
.
Therefore in the case that
k>t^{\prime}
, the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably isomorphic to a direct sum of copies of
\mathbb{Z}H_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}L}(IL^{v},\mathbb{Z})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}L}(IL^{v},\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
and
\mathbb{Z}H_{k}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}L^{\prime}}(IL^{\prime},\mathbb{Z})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}L^{\prime}}(IL^{\prime},\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
, which are both free, as needed. On the other hand, if
k\leq t^{\prime}
, then
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably isomorphic to
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}H_{k}}(IH_{k}^{v},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})\oplus\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}H_{k}}(IH_{k},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
.
The proof will be completed by showing that the latter is not a free module.

From
[
43
]
*Lemma 7.14 we know that
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}H_{k}}(IH_{k},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})\cong IH_{k}^{v}
. We will show that
IH_{k}^{v}
is not projective, which implies that

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}H_{k}}(IH_{k}^{v},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})\oplus\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}H_{k}}(IH_{k},\mathbb{Z}H_{k})

is not free. Suppose that
IH_{k}^{v}
were projective. Then
IH_{k}^{v}\to\mathbb{Z}H_{k}\to\mathbb{Z}^{v}
is a projective resolution for
\mathbb{Z}^{v}
, implying that
H_{i}(H_{k};\mathbb{Z}^{v})=0
for all
i>1
. This is a contradiction since
H_{k}=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, and its group homology with
\mathbb{Z}^{v}
coefficients can be computed by hand, writing out a 2-periodic free resolution and tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}^{v}
. This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 3.23
.

Let
\pi
be a
3
-manifold group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
Let
M
and
M^{\prime}
be closed
4
-manifolds with fundamental group
\pi
, and common orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
.
Let
c\colon M\to B\pi
and
c^{\prime}\colon M^{\prime}\to B\pi
be maps inducing the given identifications
\pi_{1}(M)\cong\pi
and
\pi_{1}(M^{\prime})\cong\pi
. Suppose that
M
and
M^{\prime}
have isomorphic quadratic
2
-types. Then
c_{*}([M])-(c^{\prime})_{*}([M^{\prime}])=0\in H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
.

As in
Propositions

3.16
and
3.22
,
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong\bigoplus_{t}H_{4}(H_{k};\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{t}
. By
Proposition

3.22
, the image
c_{*}[M]
is nontrivial in the
k
th summand if and only if
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably free.

Proof.

Since
M
and
M^{\prime}
have isomorphic quadratic
2
-types, in particular
\pi_{2}(M)\cong\pi_{2}(M^{\prime})
, and hence for each
H_{k}
factor of
\pi
, we know
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably free if and only if
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M^{\prime}),\mathbb{Z}H_{k})
is stably free.
Hence by
Proposition

3.22
the images
c_{*}([M])
and
(c^{\prime})_{*}([M^{\prime}])
agree in
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
, and so their difference vanishes.
∎

4.
P2FA and P2FA
∗
groups

The main goal of this section is to prove
Proposition

4.10
, showing that if a
4
-manifold
M
has a
3
-manifold group as fundamental group, then
\pi_{2}(M)
is free an abelian group.
We do not need to restrict to admissible
(\pi,w)
here.
We begin by introducing a relevant property of groups.

Definition 4.1
.

Let
\pi
be a finitely presented group.

(i)

A group
\pi
is
P2FA
if, for all closed
4
-manifolds
M
with fundamental group
\pi
and for all orientation characters,
\pi_{2}(M)
is free as an abelian group.

(ii)

Further, a P2FA group is said to be
P2FA
∗
if
\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
is free as an abelian group for all closed
4
-manifolds
M
with fundamental group
\pi
and for all orientation characters.

The following exact sequence will be useful in the proof of the next lemma.

Remark 4.2
.

By the universal coefficient spectral sequence
[
55
]
*Theorem 2.3,
the sequence

(4.3)

0\to H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{c^{*}}H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\ev}H_{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\to H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{c^{*}}H^{3}(X;\mathbb{Z}\pi)

is exact for any space
X
and any
2
-connected map
c\colon X\to B\pi
; see e.g.
[
32
]
*Lemma 3.3 or
[
43
]
*Proposition 3.3 for the derivation.

If
X
is a
3
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w
, then
H^{2}(X;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong H_{1}(X;\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w})=0
by Poincaré duality. In this case, it follows from the exactness of (
4.3
) that
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
.
If
X=M
is a
4
-manifold then
H^{3}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
, by Poincaré duality.

Lemma 4.4
.

A group
\pi
is P2FA if and only if
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group.

It is not known whether
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}{\pi})
is free as an abelian group for every finitely presentable group
\pi
.
There are finitely generated groups for which
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}{\pi})
is not free
[
20
]
*Chapter 13.

Proof.

By (
4.3
),
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is a subgroup of
H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi^{w})
, which is isomorphic to
\pi_{2}(M)
as an abelian group. So if
\pi
is P2FA, then
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group.

For the converse, let
(C_{*},d_{*})
be a free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
as a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
.
By
[
26
]
*Proposition 2.4
[
28
]
*Theorem 4.2
[
44
]
*Proposition 1.10,
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably an extension of
\ker d_{2}
and
\coker d^{2}
. The module
\ker d_{2}
is always free as an abelian group since it is a submodule of the free module
C_{2}
.
The extension

0\to\ker d^{3}/\operatorname{Im}d^{2}\to C^{2}/\operatorname{Im}d^{2}\to C^{2}/\ker d^{3}\to 0,

together with the fact that
C^{2}/\ker d^{3}\cong\operatorname{Im}d^{3}
, gives rise to the extension

(4.5)

0\to H^{2}(C^{*})\to\coker d^{2}\to\operatorname{Im}d^{3}\to 0.

Since
\operatorname{Im}d^{3}
is also a submodule of the free module
C^{3}
, it is free as an abelian group, and so
\coker d^{2}
, and hence
\pi_{2}(M)
, is free as an abelian group if and only if
H^{2}(C^{*})\cong H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group.
∎

Recall that a group
\pi
is said to be
FP_{n}
if there is a resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
by
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules such that the first
n
terms are finitely generated projective modules, and that
\pi
is
FP
if there is a resolution with nonzero groups in only finitely many degrees.

Lemma 4.6
.

Let
\pi
be a P2FA group. Suppose that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group or that
\pi
is
FP_{3}
. Then
\pi
is P2FA
∗
.

Proof.

Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
.
Then
M
is homotopy equivalent to a finite 4-dimensional CW complex
X
(see
[
19
]
*Theorem 3.17 for references).
We first assume that
\pi
is
FP_{3}
. Then there is a resolution
(P_{*},d_{*}^{\pi})
with
P_{i}
