a finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module for
i\leq 3
. In particular
\ker d_{2}^{\pi}=\operatorname{Im}d_{3}^{\pi}
is finitely generated.
Let
C_{*}=(C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}{\pi}),d_{*}^{X})
be the cellular chain complex of the universal cover of
X
, considered as a complex of finitely generated free left
\mathbb{Z}{\pi}
-modules.
Since
0\to\ker d_{2}^{\pi}\to P_{2}\to P_{1}\to P_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
and
0\to\ker d_{2}^{X}\to C_{2}\to C_{1}\to C_{0}\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
are both exact, with
\ker d_{2}^{\pi}
,
P_{i}
, and
C_{i}
finitely generated, it follows from Schanuel’s lemma that
\ker d_{2}^{X}
is finitely generated.
Hence
\pi_{2}(M)\cong H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}{\pi})\cong{H_{2}(C_{*})}
is finitely generated as a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module.
Let
k
be such that
\pi_{2}(M)
is a quotient of
(\mathbb{Z}{\pi})^{k}
. Then by left exactness
\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
is a subgroup of

((\mathbb{Z}{\pi})^{k})^{\dagger}\cong(\mathbb{Z}{\pi})^{k}
, so is free as an abelian group.
Thus
\pi
is P2FA
∗
.

Now we assume that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group.
As above, let
C_{*}=(C_{*}(X;\mathbb{Z}{\pi}),d_{*}^{X})
be the cellular chain complex of the universal cover of
X
, where
X
is a finite 4-dimensional CW complex homotopy equivalent to
M
.
Let
K:=X^{(2)}
be the
2
-skeleton of
X
, and consider a CW model for
B\pi
with
2
-skeleton
K
.
Let
(C_{*}^{\pi},d_{*}^{\pi})
be the free resolution of
\mathbb{Z}
as a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module obtained from the cellular chain complex of
E\pi
. Let
c\colon X\to B\pi
be a map that is the inclusion
K\to B\pi
on the
2
-skeleton. Then
c
induces a map of the chain complexes that is the identity in degrees up to two.
There is a short exact sequence

0\to\ker d^{3}_{X}\xrightarrow{(p,i)}(\ker d^{3}_{X}/\operatorname{Im}d^{2}_{X})\oplus C^{2}\xrightarrow{j-q}\coker d^{2}_{X}\to 0,

where
i,j,p,
and
q
are the canonical inclusion and projection maps. Using that
d^{2}_{\pi}=d^{2}_{X}
, this fits into the following commutative diagram, where
j^{\prime},p^{\prime},q^{\prime},
and all unlabelled arrows are again the canonical inclusion and projection maps.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker d^{3}_{\pi}/\operatorname{Im}d^{2}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker d^{3}_{\pi}/\operatorname{Im}d^{2}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker d^{3}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ker d^{3}_{X}/\operatorname{Im}d^{2}_{X})\oplus C^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker d^{2}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker d^{3}_{X}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\ker d^{3}_{X}/\ker d^{3}_{\pi})\oplus C^{2}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C^{2}/\ker d^{3}_{\pi}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(c^{*},0)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p,i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j-q}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(p^{\prime},i)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j^{\prime}-q^{\prime}}

Since
\ker d^{3}_{X}\subseteq C^{2}
and
C^{2}/\ker d^{3}_{\pi}\cong\operatorname{Im}d^{3}_{\pi}\subseteq C^{3}_{\pi}
are free as abelian groups, it follows from the bottom exact sequence that
(\ker d^{3}_{X}/\ker d^{3}_{\pi})\oplus C^{2}
is free as an abelian group. The middle vertical sequence identifies this term with
\coker c^{*}\oplus C^{2}
. Hence
\coker c^{*}
is free as an abelian group.

By (
4.3
) we have the short exact sequence

0\to\coker c^{*}\to H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\to H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to 0.

Hence
H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}
is free as an abelian group if
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group.
∎

Corollary 4.7
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a
PD_{n}
-group for some
n
. Then
\pi
is P2FA
∗
. In particular, the trivial group,
\mathbb{Z}
, and all
PD_{3}
-groups are P2FA
∗
.

Proof.

If
\pi
is a
PD_{n}
-group, then
H^{k}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong H_{n-k}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\in\{0,\mathbb{Z}\}
is free as an abelian group for all
k
.
Thus
\pi
is P2FA by
Lemma

4.4
and
is P2FA
∗
by
Lemma

4.6
.
∎

Let
\pi
be a finitely presented group. Recall that for
G\leq\pi
and a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
A
, we denote the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\mathbb{Z}G)
by
A^{\star}
. As usual, for a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
A
, we denote the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(A,\mathbb{Z}\pi)
by
A^{\dagger}
.

Lemma 4.8
.

Let
\pi
be a finitely presented group, and let
G\leq\pi
be a subgroup, let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module, and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism.
Then the map

\displaystyle\alpha\colon\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(A^{\star})

\displaystyle\to(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}A)^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\gamma\otimes f

\displaystyle\mapsto\big(\gamma\otimes a\mapsto\gamma f(a)\overline{\gamma}\big)

is an isomorphism.
As usual, we view
(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}A)^{\dagger}
as a left module using the involution
\gamma\mapsto\overline{\gamma}=w(\gamma)\gamma^{-1}
.

Proof.

The map
\alpha
is the composition

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(A^{\star})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\mathbb{Z}G)\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G}\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\mathbb{Z}G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G}\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}A)^{\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\otimes f}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\mapsto f(a))\otimes\overline{\gamma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(a\mapsto f(a)\otimes\overline{\gamma})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma\otimes a\mapsto\gamma f(a)\overline{\gamma}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Here the first isomorphism follows from the definition, switching the left module structure to a right module structure using the involution on
\mathbb{Z}\pi
. The second isomorphism uses the fact that
\mathbb{Z}\pi
is a free
\mathbb{Z}G
-module. The third isomorphism uses adjunction, and can be factored into the following three isomorphisms:

(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}A)^{\dagger}\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}A,\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\Res_{G}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\mathbb{Z}G\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G}\mathbb{Z}\pi).\qed

Lemma 4.9
.

Let
Y
be an orientable
3
-manifold and let
\pi:=\pi_{1}(Y)
.
By the prime decomposition theorem for orientable
3
-manifolds,
\pi\cong\big(*_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}\big)*\big(*_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)
, for some
k,s
, where each
L_{i}
is infinite cyclic or finite, and each
G_{j}
is a
PD_{3}
-group.
Then
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\oplus_{j=1}^{s}E_{j}
, where
E_{j}:=\mathbb{Z}\pi\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}\mathbb{Z}=\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}(\mathbb{Z})
.

Proof.

The classifying space
B\pi
splits as a wedge of the classifying spaces
BL_{i}
and
BG_{j}
. A Mayer–Vietoris argument therefore shows that

\displaystyle H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{k}H^{3}(L_{i};\Res_{L_{i}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi)\oplus\mathop{\bigoplus}_{j=1}^{s}H^{3}(G_{j};\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi)

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{k}H^{3}\big(L_{i};\mathop{\bigoplus}_{\pi/L_{i}}\mathbb{Z}L_{i}\big)\oplus\mathop{\bigoplus}_{j=1}^{s}H^{3}\big(G_{j};\mathop{\bigoplus}_{\pi/G_{j}}\mathbb{Z}G_{j}\big)

\displaystyle\cong\mathop{\bigoplus}_{i=1}^{k}\mathop{\bigoplus}_{\pi/L_{i}}H^{3}(L_{i};\mathbb{Z}L_{i})\oplus\mathop{\bigoplus}_{j=1}^{s}\mathop{\bigoplus}_{\pi/G_{j}}H^{3}(G_{j};\mathbb{Z}G_{j}).

For each
i,j
, we have
H^{3}(L_{i};\mathbb{Z}L_{i})=0
and
H^{3}(G_{j};\mathbb{Z}G_{j})\cong\mathbb{Z}
, and so

H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\mathop{\bigoplus}_{j=1}^{s}\mathop{\bigoplus}_{\pi/G_{j}}\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathop{\bigoplus}_{j=1}^{s}(\mathbb{Z}\pi\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}\mathbb{Z})=\mathop{\bigoplus}_{j=1}^{s}E_{j}

as required.
∎

Finally we prove the main result of this section showing that
3
-manifold groups are P2FA
∗
.

Proposition 4.10
.

Every
3
-manifold group is P2FA
∗
.

Proof.

Let
\pi
be the fundamental group of a
3
-manifold
Y
. Then
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
by
Remark

4.2
, so
\pi
is P2FA by
Lemma

4.4
.
Let
\pi^{\prime}:=\pi_{1}(\widehat{Y})
, where
\widehat{Y}
is the orientation double cover of
Y
. By Shapiro’s lemma,
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})
as
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}
-modules, and thus in particular as abelian groups. By
Lemma

4.9
,
H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})
is free as an abelian group, and hence so is
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
.
It now follows that
\pi
is P2FA
∗
by
Lemma

4.6
.
∎

By
Proposition

4.10
,
Theorem

1.4
(
1
) holds for the
4
-manifolds considered in
Theorem

1.4
, and the map
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
is defined. For these inferences we only need that
3
-manifold groups are P2FA. We will use the fact that they are moreover P2FA
∗
later in
Lemma

5.4
.

5.
Injectivity of
\ev^{*}

For this section, fix a finitely presented group
\pi
, a map
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
, and a connected CW complex
B
with fundamental group
\pi
. We consider the map

\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)),

induced by the evaluation map
\ev\colon H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}
. The following lemma gives a general method for showing that
\ev^{*}
is injective. Recall that
\ev^{*}
being injective is condition (
4
) of
Theorem

2.4
.

Lemma 5.1
.

If
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
, then
\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
is injective.

Proof.

As in (
4.3
), the universal coefficient spectral sequence
gives the exact sequence

H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\ev}H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\to H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi).

Since we assume
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
, the map
\ev
is surjective. We show this implies the induced map

\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))

is injective.
Let
\theta\in\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})
be such that
\ev^{*}(\theta)=0
. In other words,
\theta(\ev(x),\ev(y))=0
for all
x,y\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
. For any
a,b\in H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}
, we have
x,y\in H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
with
a=\ev(x)
and
b=\ev(y)
, since
\ev
is surjective. Hence
\theta(a,b)=\theta(\ev(x),\ev(y))=0
as claimed. Thus
\theta=0
and
\ev^{*}
is injective.
∎

Corollary 5.2
.

If
\pi
is virtually free, then
\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
is injective.

Proof.

If
\pi
is virtually free then it has a free normal subgroup
\rho
of finite index,
and
H^{q}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong{H^{q}(\rho;\mathbb{Z}\rho)}
as abelian groups,
by Shapiro’s Lemma
[
7
]
*p.73.
Since
\rho
is free these groups are 0 for all
q>1
.
Hence
Lemma

5.1
applies.
∎

However, for general
3
-manifold groups
\pi
, it can happen that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\neq 0
. Nonetheless, the conclusion still holds when
B
is the Postnikov
2
-type of a
4
-manifold with
3
-manifold fundamental group, as we show below. In fact we will show in
Proposition

5.9
that in this case
\ev^{*}
is an isomorphism. For the proof we need the following preliminary results.

Lemma 5.3
.

Suppose
\pi\cong G*H
and let
n>1
. Assume that
H^{n}(G;\mathbb{Z}G)=H^{n}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=0
. Then
H^{n}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
.

Proof.

Since
B\pi\simeq BG\vee BH
, we can see from the Mayer–Vietoris sequence that

\displaystyle H^{n}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)

\displaystyle\cong H^{n}(G;\Res_{G}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi)\oplus H^{n}(H;\Res_{H}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi)

\displaystyle\cong H^{n}\Big(G;\bigoplus_{\pi/G}\mathbb{Z}G\Big)\oplus H^{n}\Big(H;\bigoplus_{\pi/H}\mathbb{Z}H\Big)

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{\pi/G}H^{n}(G;\mathbb{Z}G)\oplus\bigoplus_{\pi/H}H^{n}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=0.\qed

Let
\pi
be a group. Recall that, for a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
A
, we denote the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(A,\mathbb{Z}\pi)
by
A^{\dagger}
. For
G\leq\pi
and a
\mathbb{Z}G
-module
A
, we denote the module
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(A,\mathbb{Z}G)
by
A^{\star}
. In the proof below we will have two subgroups
G,H\leq\pi
, and we will consider both
\mathbb{Z}G
-duals and
\mathbb{Z}H
-duals. To avoid a proliferation of symbols, we will use the same notation in both cases.

Lemma 5.4
.

Let
G
be a
PD_{3}
-group and let
\pi=G*H
for some
3
-manifold group
H
. Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
. Then
\Res_{G}^{\pi}\big(\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}\big)
is projective.

Proof.

By
Lemma

3.13
,
M
is stably homeomorphic to
M_{1}\#M_{2}
with
\pi_{1}(M_{1})\cong G
and
\pi_{1}(M_{2})\cong H
. Hence
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to

\Ind_{G}^{\pi}\pi_{2}(M_{1})\oplus\Ind_{H}^{\pi}\pi_{2}(M_{2}).

Therefore,
\Res_{G}^{\pi}\big(\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}\big)
is stably isomorphic to

(5.5)

\Res^{\pi}_{G}\Big(\big(\Ind^{\pi}_{G}\pi_{2}(M_{1})\oplus\Ind^{\pi}_{H}\pi_{2}(M_{2})\big)^{\dagger}\Big)\cong\Res^{\pi}_{G}\big((\Ind^{\pi}_{G}\pi_{2}(M_{1}))^{\dagger}\big)\oplus\Res^{\pi}_{G}\big((\Ind^{\pi}_{H}\pi_{2}(M_{2}))^{\dagger}\big)

We consider the first summand. By
Lemma

4.8
we have that
(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}\pi_{2}(M_{1}))^{\dagger}\cong\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(\pi_{2}(M_{1})^{\star})
, and hence

\Res_{G}^{\pi}(\Ind_{G}^{\pi}\pi_{2}(M_{1}))^{\dagger}\cong\Res_{G}^{\pi}\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(\pi_{2}(M_{1})^{\star}),

where
\pi_{2}(M_{1})^{\star}:=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G}(\pi_{2}(M_{1}),\mathbb{Z}G)
.
By
Corollary

3.11
,
\pi_{2}(M_{1})^{\star}
is a stably free
\mathbb{Z}G
-module, hence
\Res_{G}^{\pi}\Ind_{G}^{\pi}(\pi_{2}(M_{1})^{\star})
is projective (but not necessarily finitely generated).
This deals with the first summand of (
5.5
), and so it remains to consider
\Res_{G}^{\pi}\big((\Ind_{H}^{\pi}\pi_{2}(M_{2}))^{\dagger}\big)
.

Again using
Lemma

4.8
,
\Res_{G}^{\pi}\big((\Ind_{H}^{\pi}\pi_{2}(M_{2}))^{\dagger}\big)\cong\Res_{G}^{\pi}\Ind_{H}^{\pi}(\pi_{2}(M_{2})^{\star})
, where
\pi_{2}(M_{2})^{\star}:=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}H}(\pi_{2}(M_{2}),\mathbb{Z}H)
. By
Proposition

4.10
we know that
\pi_{2}(M_{2})^{\star}
is free as an abelian group. We will finish the proof by showing that
\Res_{G}^{\pi}\Ind_{H}^{\pi}A
is free for every
\mathbb{Z}H
-module
A
that is free as an abelian group.
By
[
7
]
*Proposition III.5.6(b),

\Res_{G}^{\pi}\Ind_{H}^{\pi}A
is isomorphic to a direct sum of modules of the form
\Ind_{\{e\}}^{G}\Res_{\{e\}}^{H}A
, which are again free, since
\Res_{\{e\}}^{H}A
is just
A
considered as an abelian group.
Therefore we have shown that each summand of (
5.5
) is projective, which completes the proof.
∎

The following lemma concerns general
3
-manifold groups, without an admissibility assumption.

Lemma 5.6
.

Suppose that
\pi
is a
3
-manifold group.
Let
B=P_{2}(M)
, where
M
is a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
.
Let
A=\mathbb{Z}\pi
or
A=\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
. Then

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},A)\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)

is injective.

Proof.

Since
B
can be constructed from
M
by only adding cells of dimension four and higher, the map
M\to P_{2}(M)=B
induces isomorphisms
\pi_{1}(M)\xrightarrow{\cong}\pi_{1}(B)
,
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\xrightarrow{\cong}H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}
, and
H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
. By naturality of the evaluation map, we obtain the exact sequence

(5.7)

0\to H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\ev}H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\to H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to 0

from the exact sequence (
4.3
), where we used
H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
and
H^{3}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
as mentioned.
Apply the
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(-,A)
functor to obtain the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)}

We need to show that
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)
is an injection. For this, we will show that the left term
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)
vanishes.

Let
Y
be a
3
-manifold such that
\pi\cong\pi_{1}(Y)
and let
\pi^{\prime}=\pi_{1}(\widehat{Y})
, where
\widehat{Y}
is the orientation double cover of
Y
. By Shapiro’s lemma,
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})
as
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}
-modules. Hence
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)
is a submodule of
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}),A^{\prime})
, where
A^{\prime}:=\Res_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\pi}(A)
.
By the prime decomposition theorem for orientable
3
-manifolds,
\pi^{\prime}\cong\big(*_{i=1}^{k}L_{i}\big)*\big(*_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)
, for some
k,s
, where each
L_{i}
is infinite cyclic or finite, and each
G_{j}
is a
PD_{3}
-group.

By
Lemma

4.9
,
H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})\cong\oplus_{j=1}^{s}E_{j}
, where
E_{j}=\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}\mathbb{Z}=\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z})
. Hence

\displaystyle\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}),A^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(\oplus_{j=1}^{s}E_{j},A^{\prime})\cong\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(E_{j},A^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z},A^{\prime})\cong\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}(\mathbb{Z},\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A).

Let
\widehat{M}
be the double cover of
M
corresponding to
\pi^{\prime}\leq\pi
.
As
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}
-modules we have

\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\Ind^{\pi}_{\pi^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}))\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(M),\Coind_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(\pi_{2}(\widehat{M}),\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}),

where
\Coind_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}\cong\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(\mathbb{Z}\pi,\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})
is the coinduction of
\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}
, and for the middle isomorphism we used that
\Ind^{\pi}_{\pi^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}\cong\Coind_{\pi^{\prime}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}

[
7
]
*Proposition III.5.9. The last isomorphism used the
\Res-\Coind
adjunction and that
\Res^{\pi}_{\pi^{\prime}}\pi_{2}(M)\cong\pi_{2}(\widehat{M})
.
For
A=\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
it follows that

\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A\cong\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi^{\prime}}A^{\prime}\cong\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi^{\prime}}(\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\pi_{2}(\widehat{M}),\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime})).

Hence
\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A
is projective by
Lemma

5.4
. For
A=\mathbb{Z}\pi
, the module
\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A
is projective as well.

Since
G_{j}
is infinite, the only
G_{j}
-fixed point in
\mathbb{Z}G_{j}
is the trivial element.
Since
\Res^{\pi}_{G_{j}}A
is projective,
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}(\mathbb{Z},\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A)\subseteq\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}(\mathbb{Z},\bigoplus\mathbb{Z}G_{j})=0
. Thus
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}}(H^{3}(\pi^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}\pi^{\prime}),A^{\prime})=0
and hence also
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)=0
as claimed.
∎

Lemma 5.8
.

Let
\pi\cong H*\big(*_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)
such that
H^{2}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=H^{3}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=0
and each
G_{j}
is a
PD_{3}
-group. Let
B=P_{2}(M)
, where
M
is a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
. Let
A=\mathbb{Z}\pi
or
A=\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
,
then

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},A)\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)

is surjective.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Lemma

5.6
, there is a short exact sequence

0\to H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\ev}H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\to H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to 0.

Apply the
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(-,A)
functor to obtain the exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)}

We need to show that
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)
is a surjection. For this, we will show that the term
\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)
vanishes. We have
H^{3}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=0
and
H^{3}(G_{j};\mathbb{Z}G_{j})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{w(G_{j})}
, where
w(G_{j})\colon G_{j}\to C_{2}
is the orientation character of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
G_{j}
. A Mayer–Vietoris argument as in the proof of
Lemma

4.9
shows that
H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\oplus_{j=1}^{s}E_{j}
, where
E_{j}=\mathbb{Z}\pi\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}\mathbb{Z}^{w(G_{j})}=\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{w(G_{j})}\big)
. Thus
\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)
is isomorphic to

\displaystyle\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{3}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi),A)

\displaystyle\cong\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\oplus_{j=1}^{s}E_{j},A)\cong\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(E_{j},A)

\displaystyle\cong\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\big(\Ind_{G_{j}}^{\pi}\mathbb{Z}^{w(G_{j})},A\big)\cong\prod_{j=1}^{s}\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{w(G_{j})},\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A\big),

where we again used the Ind-Res adjunction.
We consider each factor separately. By Poincaré duality, we have

\Ext^{1}_{\mathbb{Z}G_{j}}\big(\mathbb{Z}^{w(G_{j})},\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A\big)\cong H^{1}\big(G_{j};(\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A)^{w(G_{j})}\big)\cong H_{2}(G_{j};\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A).

Since
\Res_{G_{j}}^{\pi}A
is projective as in the proof of
Lemma

5.6
this is trivial. Hence
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,A)
is surjective as claimed.
∎

Proposition 5.9
.

Let
\pi
be a
3
-manifold group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism. Let
B=P_{2}(M)
, where
M
is a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
.
Then
\ev^{*}\colon\Her^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))
is injective. If moreover
(\pi,w)
is admissible, then
\ev^{*}
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

We know

\ev^{\dagger}:=\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,\mathbb{Z}\pi)\colon H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger\dagger}\to H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}

is injective by setting
A=\mathbb{Z}\pi
in
Lemma

5.6
. Apply the functor
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},-)
, which is covariant and left-exact, to see that

(\ev^{\dagger})_{*}\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger\dagger})\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})

is injective. On the other hand, we can apply
Lemma

5.6
using
A=H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger}\cong\pi_{2}(M)^{\dagger}
to see that

\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\colon\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi),H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})

is injective.
As a consequence, the composition
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})\circ(\ev^{\dagger})_{*}
in the top row of the commuting diagram

(5.10)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger},H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi),H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sesq^{w}(H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Sesq^{w}(H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

is injective. Hence the bottom horizontal map is also injective.

Recall that Hermitian forms are the
\Sigma_{2}
-fixed points of sesquilinear forms. So
\ev^{*}
is the restriction of the bottom horizontal map in diagram (
5.10
) to Hermitian forms. Since the bottom horizontal map is
\Sigma_{2}
-equivariant, the first part of the proposition, that
\ev^{*}
is injective, follows by taking
\Sigma_{2}
-fixed points.

Now suppose that
(\pi,w)
is admissible. Then
\pi
is a free product of groups that are
cyclic, isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, or
PD_{3}
-groups, as in (
3.15
). If
G
is cyclic or isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, then
H^{2}(G;\mathbb{Z}G)=H^{3}(G;\mathbb{Z}G)=0
. Write
\pi=H*\big(*_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)
, where each
G_{j}
is a
PD_{3}
-group and
H
is the free product of all the remaining factors. By
Lemma

5.3
we see that
H^{2}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=H^{3}(H;\mathbb{Z}H)=0
. Thus
\ev^{\dagger}
and
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\ev,H^{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{\dagger})
are surjective by
Lemma

5.8
. Arguing as above, this implies that
(\ev^{\dagger})_{*}
, and therefore
\ev^{*}
, is an isomorphism, as claimed.
∎

In particular,
Proposition

5.9
shows that
Theorem

2.4
(
4
) holds for
4
-manifolds with
3
-manifold fundamental group, with no admissibility condition on subgroups or the orientation character.

6.
Injectivity results for
\mathcal{B}

Again, we fix a finitely presented group
\pi
and a map
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
. In this section we give general criteria under which the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
is injective, where
A
is a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module that is free as an abelian group. The map
\mathcal{B}_{A}
was defined in (
2.2
).
Note that if
A
is finitely generated and projective it is a subgroup of
\oplus^{k}\mathbb{Z}\pi
for some
k
, and hence is free as an abelian group. So the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is defined in this case.

We start the section with the following special case of
[
34
]
*Theorem 1.

Proposition 6.1

(
[
34
]
*Theorem 1)
.

Let
A
be a finitely generated projective
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Assume that there is no
g\in\pi
of order two with
w(g)=-1
. Then the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
is injective.

The following definition appeared in
[
30
]
*Definition 6.4
[
2
]
*§4.4, pp. 476-477.

Definition 6.2
.

A
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
L
is called
torsionless
if there exists a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-embedding
L\to F
where
F
is a finitely generated, free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. The module
L
is called
w
-strongly torsionless
if additionally the induced map
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(L)\to\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(F)
is injective.

Note that an arbitrary torsionless
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
A
is free as an abelian group, since it is a subgroup of
\oplus^{k}\mathbb{Z}\pi
for some
k
, which is free as an abelian group. Therefore the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is defined. We will use this fact in the sequel without comment.

Proposition 6.3
.

Let
L
be a
w
-strongly torsionless
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Assume that there is no
g\in\pi
of order two with
w(g)=-1
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{L}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(L)\to\Her^{w}(L^{\dagger})
is injective.

Proof.

Let
L\xrightarrow{\theta}F
be an embedding as in
Definition

6.2
. Consider the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her^{w}(L^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(F)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her^{w}(F^{\dagger}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{L}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{F}}

The map
\theta_{*}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(L)\to\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(F)
is injective by assumption and the map
\mathcal{B}_{F}
is injective by
Proposition

6.1
. Hence
\mathcal{B}_{L}
is injective as claimed.
∎

It is immediate from the definition (
Definition

3.1
) that twisted augmentation ideals are torsionless. We now show that they are moreover
w
-strongly torsionless. The case of
\pi
finite and
v
trivial was done in
[
26
]
*Lemma 2.3.

Lemma 6.4
.

Let
v\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism. There is an isomorphism

\Gamma(I\pi^{v})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi\cong\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi),

which on
\Gamma(I\pi^{v})
is induced by the inclusion
I\pi^{v}\to\mathbb{Z}\pi
. In particular,
I\pi^{v}
is
w
-strongly torsionless.

Proof.

As
\mathbb{Z}\pi
is free as an abelian group,
\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is isomorphic to the group of symmetric elements of
\mathbb{Z}\pi\otimes\mathbb{Z}\pi
. Therefore
\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
has a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis
\{g\otimes g,g\otimes h+h\otimes g\mid g,h\in\pi,g\neq h\}
. We also have an inclusion
\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
induced by
1\mapsto 1\otimes 1
. Similarly
\Gamma(I\pi^{v})
consists of the symmetric elements of
I\pi^{v}\otimes I\pi^{v}
.

By sending
g\otimes h+h\otimes g
to
-v(gh)\big(v(g)g-v(h)h)\otimes(v(g)g-v(h)h)\big)
we obtain a map

\theta\colon\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)/\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\Gamma(I\pi^{v}).

To see this, since
g\otimes h+h\otimes g
for
g\neq h
is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis of
\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)/\mathbb{Z}\pi
, it suffices to show that the map is
\pi
-equivariant, which is a straightforward verification. Recall that the
\pi
action on both
\Gamma
groups is diagonal, and the action is not twisted by
v
.

The map
\Gamma(I\pi^{v})\to\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
induced by the inclusion
I\pi^{v}\to\mathbb{Z}\pi
yields a map
\psi\colon\Gamma(I\pi^{v})\to\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)/\mathbb{Z}\pi
. We have

-v(gh)\big((v(g)g-v(h)h)\otimes(v(g)g-v(h)h)\big)=h\otimes g+g\otimes h-v(gh)h\otimes h-v(gh)g\otimes g.

Hence
\psi\circ\theta
is the identity on
\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)/\mathbb{Z}\pi
because
g\otimes g
and
h\otimes h
lie in
\mathbb{Z}\pi\subseteq\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
. So
\theta
is injective.

We will show below that
\theta
is also surjective. This will imply that we have the following commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)/\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Gamma(I\pi^{v})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}}

where
i_{*}
denotes the inclusion-induced map. Thus
i_{*}
provides a splitting of the short exact sequence, and we deduce that
\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\Gamma(I\pi^{v})\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi
.

We complete the argument by showing that the map
\theta
is surjective. Note that
\{v(g)g-v(h)h\mid g,h\in\pi\}
gives a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
I\pi^{v}
, and therefore

\displaystyle\{(v(g)g-v(h)h)\otimes v(g)g-v(h)h\mid g,h\in\pi\}

\displaystyle\cup\{(v(g)g-v(h)h)\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})\otimes(v(g)g-v(h)h)\mid g,g^{\prime},h,h^{\prime}\in\pi\}

is a
\mathbb{Z}
-basis for
\Gamma(I\pi^{v})
. By definition of
\theta
,

\theta\big(-v(gh)(g\otimes h+h\otimes g)\big)=v(g)g-v(h)h\otimes v(g)g-v(h)h,

for
g,h\in\pi
. So it remains to show that elements of the form

(6.5)

(v(g)g-v(h)h)\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})\otimes(v(g)g-v(h)h)

lie in the image of
\theta
.
Set
\widetilde{g}:=h^{-1}g
,
\widetilde{g}^{\prime}:=h^{-1}g^{\prime}
, and
\widetilde{h}^{\prime}:=h^{-1}h^{\prime}
. Then we rewrite (
6.5
) as

(v(h\widetilde{g})h\widetilde{g}-v(h)h)\otimes(v(h\widetilde{g}^{\prime})h\widetilde{g}^{\prime}-v(h\widetilde{h}^{\prime})h\widetilde{h}^{\prime})+(v(h\widetilde{g}^{\prime})h\widetilde{g}^{\prime}-v(h\widetilde{h}^{\prime})h\widetilde{h}^{\prime})\otimes(v(h\widetilde{g})h\widetilde{g}-v(h)h).

Since the image of
\theta
is a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-submodule, we can act by
v(h)h^{-1}
, and it suffices to show that the resulting elements, which are of the following form, lie in
\operatorname{Im}\theta
:

(v(\widetilde{g})\widetilde{g}-1)\otimes(v(\widetilde{g}^{\prime})\widetilde{g}^{\prime}-v(\widetilde{h}^{\prime})\widetilde{h}^{\prime})+(v(\widetilde{g}^{\prime})\widetilde{g}^{\prime}-v(\widetilde{h}^{\prime})\widetilde{h}^{\prime})\otimes(v(\widetilde{g})\widetilde{g}-1).

For readability, and since we consider arbitrary
g
,
g^{\prime}
, and
h^{\prime}
, we drop the tildes from the notation and consider:

\displaystyle(v(g)

\displaystyle g-1)\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})\otimes(v(g)g-1)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(v(g)g-1)\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)+(v(g)g-1)\otimes(1-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})

\displaystyle+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)\otimes(v(g)g-1)+(1-v(h^{\prime})h^{\prime})\otimes(v(g)g-1).

Hence it suffices to show that elements of the form

(6.6)

(v(g)g-1)\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)\otimes(v(g)g-1)

lie in
\operatorname{Im}\theta
. Since

\displaystyle(1-v(g)g)\otimes(v(g)g-1)

\displaystyle=\theta\big(-v(g)(1\otimes g+g\otimes 1)\big)\text{ and }

\displaystyle(1-v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime})\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)

\displaystyle=\theta\big(-v(g^{\prime})(1\otimes g^{\prime}+g^{\prime}\otimes 1)\big)

are in the image of
\theta
by definition, we can add these to (
6.6
), to obtain the following expression, which lies in
\operatorname{Im}\theta
if and only if (
6.6
) does.

\displaystyle(v(g)

\displaystyle g-1)\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)\otimes(v(g)g-1)

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle(1-v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime})\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)+(1-v(g)g)\otimes(v(g)g-1)

\displaystyle=(v(g)g-v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime})\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-1)+(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(g)g)\otimes(v(g)g-1)

\displaystyle=(v(g)g-v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime})\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(g)g).

But
\theta\big(-v(gg^{\prime})(g\otimes g^{\prime}+g^{\prime}\otimes g)\big)=(v(g)g-v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime})\otimes(v(g^{\prime})g^{\prime}-v(g)g)
, so this lies in the image of
\theta
, and thus indeed (
6.6
) lies in
\operatorname{Im}\theta
.
This completes the proof that
\theta
is surjective, and hence an isomorphism.

For the final sentence of the lemma note that since
\Gamma(I\pi^{v})
is a summand of
\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
, it follows that
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(I\pi^{v})
is also a summand of
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
. So
I\pi^{v}
is
w
-strongly torsionless, with
F=\mathbb{Z}\pi
, as desired.
∎

Corollary 6.7
.

Assume that there is no
g\in\pi
of order two with
w(g)=-1
.
Let
v\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism.
The map
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{v}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(I\pi^{v})\to\Her^{w}((I\pi^{v})^{\dagger})
is injective.

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.4
,
I\pi^{v}
is
w
-strongly torsionless. Hence
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{v}}
is injective by
Proposition

6.3
.
∎

Corollary

6.7
shows that
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is injective for
A
a twisted augmentation ideal, assuming that
w
is trivial on elements of order two. Soon, in
Corollary

6.13
, we generalise this to show that
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is also injective whenever
A
is stably isomorphic to such a twisted augmentation ideal.

We will use the following lemma due to Baues.
In the statement, to form
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}
, we consider
A
as a right
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module using the involution on
\mathbb{Z}\pi
given by
g\mapsto w(g)g^{-1}
.

Lemma 6.8

(
[
4
]
*(1.2.7))
.

Let
\pi
be a group and let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules that are free as abelian groups. Let
\iota_{A}\colon A\to A\oplus A^{\prime}
and
\iota_{A^{\prime}}\colon A^{\prime}\to A\oplus A^{\prime}
be the canonical inclusions, and define

\displaystyle\psi\colon A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime})

\displaystyle a\otimes a^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto a\otimes a^{\prime}+a^{\prime}\otimes a

where we use the description of
\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime})
as the symmetric elements in
(A\oplus A^{\prime})\otimes(A\oplus A^{\prime})
.
Then

\Gamma(\iota_{A})\oplus\Gamma(\iota_{A^{\prime}})\oplus\psi\colon\Gamma(A)\oplus\Gamma(A^{\prime})\oplus A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}A^{\prime}\xrightarrow{\cong}\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime})

is an isomorphism.

Remark 6.9
.

Furthermore the map

\Her^{w}((A\oplus A^{\prime})^{\dagger})\xrightarrow{\cong}\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})\oplus\Her^{w}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger})\oplus\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},A^{\dagger\dagger}),

induced by the restrictions along the inclusions of
A^{\dagger}
and
(A^{\prime})^{\dagger}
into
(A\oplus A^{\prime})^{\dagger}
, is an isomorphism.

Lemma 6.10
.

Let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be
(
left
)

\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules that are free as abelian groups. Then with respect to the decomposition of
\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime})
from
Lemma

6.8
, and the decomposition of
\Her^{w}((A\oplus A^{\prime})^{\dagger})
from
Remark

6.9
, the map
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus A^{\prime}}
is isomorphic to the direct sum of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{A^{\prime}}
, and the map

\displaystyle A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}

\displaystyle\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},A^{\dagger\dagger})

\displaystyle a\otimes a^{\prime}

\displaystyle\mapsto(f\mapsto(g\mapsto\overline{g(a)}f(a^{\prime}))).

Remark 6.11
.

Here and throughout we adopt the convention that the tensor product
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}
of two left
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules
A
and
A^{\prime}
is formed by converting
A
into a right
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module using the involution, and then taking the tensor product.
We will use the same convention shortly when forming
\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(A,A^{\prime})
.

Proof.

The splittings of the domain and codomain, from
Lemma

6.8
and
Remark

6.9
respectively, induce the claimed splitting of
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus A^{\prime}}
. We only show this for the image of
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}
and leave the other cases to the reader. Here
a\otimes a^{\prime}
maps under
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus A^{\prime}}
to the Hermitian form on
(A\oplus A^{\prime})^{\dagger}
given by
((f_{1},g_{1}),(f_{2},g_{2}))\mapsto\overline{g_{1}(a^{\prime})}f_{2}(a)
. In particular, this is trivial when restricted to
\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
and
\Her^{w}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger})
. Hence it maps to
\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},A^{\dagger\dagger})
, and this is given by the map claimed.
∎

Lemma 6.12
.

Assume that there is no
g\in\pi
of order two with
w(g)=-1
.
Let
A
be a torsionless
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module and let
k\geq 0
. Then the canonical map from the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
to the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k})\to\Her^{w}((A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k})^{\dagger})
is an isomorphism.

Proof.

Since
A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k}
is again torsionless, it suffices to consider the case
k=1
by induction. Let
A\to F
be an embedding into a finitely generated, free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module.
By the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A^{\dagger\dagger}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F^{\dagger\dagger},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

the map
\ev_{A}
sending
a\in A
to
(f\mapsto\overline{f(a)})\in A^{\dagger\dagger}
is injective.

By
Lemma

6.10
,
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi}
is given by the direct sum of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}
, and
\ev_{A}
.
By the previous paragraph
\ev_{A}
is injective and
\mathcal{B}_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}
is injective by
Proposition

6.1
. It follows that the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is isomorphic to the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k}}
, as claimed.
∎

Corollary 6.13
.

Assume that there is no
g\in\pi
of order two with
w(g)=-1
.
Let
v\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism and let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Suppose there exists
k,j\geq 0
such that
A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k}\cong I\pi^{v}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{j}
. Then
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is defined and injective.

Proof.

Note that by hypothesis
A
is a subgroup of
(\mathbb{Z}\pi)^{j+1}
and is therefore free as an abelian group. Thus the map
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is defined. By
Lemma

6.12
, we know that the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is isomorphic to the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k}}
, via a natural map. By hypothesis this kernel can be identified with that of
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{v}\oplus\mathbb{Z}\pi^{k}}
. Another application of
Lemma

6.12
shows this is isomorphic to the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{v}}
, which is trivial by
Corollary

6.7
.
∎

Lemma 6.14
.

Let
A
and
A^{\prime}
be torsionless
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-modules and suppose that
\mathcal{B}_{A}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)\to\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})
and
\mathcal{B}_{A^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A^{\prime})\to\Her^{w}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger})
are injective. Assume that
0\to A\xrightarrow{j}F\to Q\to 0
is exact with
F
a finitely generated, free
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module. Then the kernel of

\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus A^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime})\to\Her^{w}((A\oplus A^{\prime})^{\dagger})

is contained in the image of

\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(Q,A^{\prime})\to A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}\leq\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime}).

Proof.

By
Lemma

6.10
,
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus A^{\prime}}
is the direct sum of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
,
\mathcal{B}_{A^{\prime}}
, and
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}\to\Hom((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},A^{\dagger\dagger})
.
Since
\mathcal{B}_{A}
and
\mathcal{B}_{A^{\prime}}
are assumed to be injective, the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A\oplus A^{\prime}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A\oplus A^{\prime})\to\Her^{w}((A\oplus A^{\prime})^{\dagger})
is the kernel of
A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}\to\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},A^{\dagger\dagger})
.
Consider the following commuting diagram, where the latter map is the left vertical map:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(Q,A^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces A\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces F\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}A^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},A^{\dagger\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}((A^{\prime})^{\dagger},F^{\dagger\dagger}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j\otimes\operatorname{Id}_{A^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Hom(-,j^{\dagger\dagger})}

Since
F
is free, the right hand vertical map can be identified with a sum of copies of
\ev_{A^{\prime}}
.
Since
\ev_{A^{\prime}}\colon A^{\prime}\to(A^{\prime})^{\dagger\dagger}
is injective as in the proof of
Lemma

6.12
, the right hand vertical map is injective. The proof is then completed by a diagram chase.
∎

We end this section with the following result, which we will need in
Sections

8
and
10
.

Proposition 6.15
.

Let
\pi
be a finite group and let
A
be a
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module that is free as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module. Then the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
equals the torsion subgroup of
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)
.

Proof.

For any
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module
B
that is free as a
\mathbb{Z}
-module, there is a map

\Phi_{B}\circ\overline{\omega}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(B^{\dagger})\to\Her^{w}(B)

induced by
f\otimes g\mapsto((a,b)\mapsto f(a)\overline{g(b)})
. Our notation corresponds to
[
43
]
*Lemma 4.19, where it was shown that the kernel of
\Phi_{B}\circ\overline{\omega}
is the torsion in
\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(B^{\dagger})
. Note that
[
43
]
*Lemma 4.19 is stated for a special
\mathbb{Z}\pi
-module and
w=0
. However this proof can be adapted in a straightforward way to show the statement claimed by replacing the standard norm element with the twisted norm element
\sum_{g\in\pi}w(g)g
. We apply this with
B=A^{\dagger}
below.

By
[
57
]
*Lemma 2.5,
A
is reflexive, i.e. the map
\ev\colon A\to A^{\dagger\dagger}
given by
a\mapsto(f\mapsto\overline{f(a)})
is an isomorphism. Considering the commutative diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A^{\dagger\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her^{w}(A^{\dagger})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Phi_{A^{\dagger}}\circ\overline{\omega}}

we see that the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{A}
is the torsion in
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Gamma(A)
as claimed.
∎

7.
Proving
Theorem

2.4
(
3
) for
3
-manifold fundamental groups

Let
\pi
be a
3
-manifold group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism with
(\pi,w)
admissible.
The goal of this section is to work towards proving
Theorem

2.4
(
3
) for
4
-manifolds
M
with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w
.
Recall that this condition states:
the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon
is contained in the kernel of
\varphi_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
, where
B
is the Postnikov
2
-type of
M
. We recall that by
Proposition

4.10
,
H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is free as an abelian group, so the map
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
is defined.

In
Section

7.1
we obtain upper bounds on the size of the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
.
In
Section

7.2
we focus on the group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, and we obtain lower bounds on the size of the kernel of
\varphi_{B}
, when
B
is the
2
-type of a
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, for a specific stable isomorphism class of
\pi_{2}(M)
.
In
Section

7.3
we again consider
4
-manifolds
M
of type
(\pi,w)
. For
B=P_{2}(M)
we use the results from
Section

7.2
to obtain analogous lower bounds on the size of the kernel of
\varphi_{B}
for all the fundamental groups considered in
Theorem

1.4
.

The output of this section is summarised in
Corollary

7.10
. As explained in the proof of that corollary, we know that
\ker\varphi_{B}
is contained in
\ker\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon
in general. Our upper bounds from
Section

7.1
and lower bounds from
Section

7.3
coincide, and we deduce in
Corollary

7.10
that
\ker(\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon)=\ker\varphi_{B}
in our setting. Of course it then follows that
\ker(\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\circ\Upsilon)\subseteq\ker\varphi_{B}
, so
Theorem

2.4
(
3
) is satisfied.

7.1.
Upper bounds on the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
for
3
-manifold fundamental groups

As in
Section

3.3
, for the remainder of this section, let
\pi
be a
3
-manifold group and let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism with
(\pi,w)
admissible. Then, by the prime decomposition theorem for
3
-manifolds, we know there is a decomposition of the form

(7.1)

\pi\cong F*\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t}H_{k}\big),

for some
r,s,t\geq 0
, with
F
a free group,
Z_{i}
a finite cyclic group for each
i
,
G_{j}
a
PD_{3}
-group for each
j
and
H_{k}\cong\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
for each
k
.

Proposition 7.2
.

Fix a decomposition for
\pi
as in (
7.1
). Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi

and orientation character
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
such that
(\pi,w)
is admissible.
By reordering the factors of
\pi
if needed, we assume that there exists
t^{\prime}\leq t
such that the image of the fundamental class
[M]
in
H_{4}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t}H_{4}(H_{k};\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{t}
is trivial in the first
t^{\prime}
summands and nontrivial for
k>t^{\prime}
.
Then the kernel of
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
is isomorphic to
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\tau}
for some
\tau\leq t^{\prime}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

3.16
,
H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\cong\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}(I\Gamma^{v})\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}(I\Gamma^{\prime})
, where

\Gamma=\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{j=1}^{s}G_{j}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{k}\big)

and

\Gamma^{\prime}=\big(\ast_{i=1}^{r}Z_{i}\big)*\big(\ast_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{k}\big)

are subgroups of
\pi
in the canonical way. Here
v=wv^{\prime}\colon\Gamma\to C_{2}
, where
v^{\prime}
is trivial on each
Z_{i}
, is the projection onto the second factor on
\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
(followed by the canonical isomorphism
\mathbb{Z}/2\to C_{2}
), and on each
G_{j}
factor it is the orientation character
u_{j}
of the aspherical
PD_{3}
-complex with fundamental group
G_{j}
. By
Lemma

6.12
we therefore have

\ker\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\cong\ker\mathcal{B}_{\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}}.

Extend
v\colon\Gamma\to C_{2}
to
\overline{v}\colon\pi\to C_{2}
by taking the trivial map on the factors of
\pi
not in
\Gamma
.
By
Lemma

3.12
,
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\leq I\pi^{\overline{v}}
and
\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}\leq I\pi
, where each inclusion is of a summand. Similarly, for the inclusion
\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}\leq I\pi
, we extend the trivial map on
\Gamma^{\prime}
to the trivial map on
\pi
. Since
\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
is a summand of
I\pi^{\overline{v}}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
, by
Lemma

6.10
we have that

\ker\mathcal{B}_{\Ind_{\Gamma}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{v}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}}\leq\ker\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{\overline{v}}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}}

under the natural inclusion from that lemma.

By
Corollary

6.7
, the map
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{u}}
is injective for every twisted augmentation ideal
I\pi^{u}
, where
u\colon\pi\to C_{2}
is an arbitrary homomorphism that vanishes on elements of
\pi
of order two.
Applying this to
u=\overline{v}
, we see that
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{\overline{v}}}
is injective. Also, we have that
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi}
is injective, by setting
u
to be the trivial map. It then follows from
Lemma

6.10
and the fact that
\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
is a summand of
I\pi
that
\mathcal{B}_{\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}}
is also injective.

Now we want to apply
Lemma

6.14
with
0\to A\to F\to Q\to 0
as
0\to I\pi^{\overline{v}}\to\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\mathbb{Z}^{\overline{v}}\to 0
and
A^{\prime}=\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}
. The
lemma applies because
\mathcal{B}_{I\pi^{\overline{v}}}
and
\mathcal{B}_{\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}}
are injective. We deduce using this and the two previous displayed equations that

\ker\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(M;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}\leq\ker B_{I\pi^{\overline{v}}\oplus\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}}\leq\operatorname{Im}\big(\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\mathbb{Z}^{\overline{v}},\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime})\to I\pi^{\overline{v}}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime}\big).

Then by the definition, Shapiro’s lemma, and dimension shifting, we have

\displaystyle\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\mathbb{Z}^{\overline{v}},\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime})

\displaystyle\cong H_{1}(\pi;(\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime})^{w\overline{v}})\cong H_{1}(\Gamma^{\prime};(I\Gamma^{\prime})^{v^{\prime}})\cong H_{2}(\Gamma^{\prime};\mathbb{Z}^{v^{\prime}})

\displaystyle\cong\bigoplus_{i=1}^{r}H_{2}(Z_{i};\mathbb{Z})\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}H_{2}(H_{k};\mathbb{Z}^{v^{\prime}})\cong 0\oplus\bigoplus_{k=1}^{t^{\prime}}\mathbb{Z}/2\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{t^{\prime}}.

Here the first isomorphism makes use of
Remark

6.11
, which says that the
w
-twisted involution is implicitly used in defining
\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\mathbb{Z}^{\overline{v}},\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime})
; to incorporate this into the group homology
H_{1}(\pi;(\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime})^{w\overline{v}})
, we must give the coefficients an extra
w
-twisting.
Since the image of
\Tor_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}\pi}(\mathbb{Z}^{\overline{v}},\Ind_{\Gamma^{\prime}}^{\pi}I\Gamma^{\prime})\cong(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{t^{\prime}}
under a homomorphism is
(\mathbb{Z}/2)^{\tau}
for some
\tau\leq t^{\prime}
, this completes the proof.
∎

7.2.
Lower bounds on the kernel of
\varphi_{B}
for fundamental group

\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2

For this subsection let
\pi=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2=\langle t,T\mid[T,t],T^{2}\rangle
and let
v^{\prime}\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be given by
v^{\prime}(t)=1
and
v^{\prime}(T)=-1
. In other words,
v^{\prime}
is trivial on the
\mathbb{Z}
factor and nontrivial on the
\mathbb{Z}/2
factor.

We first consider the case of a specific Postnikov
2
-type
P
with fundamental group
\mathbb{Z}/2
. Note that this need not be the Postnikov
2
-type of a
4
-manifold. This will a useful ingredient in
Lemmas

7.5
and
7.5
where we consider a Postnikov
2
-type
B
with fundamental group
\pi
, by comparing with
P
.

Let
\mathbb{Z}^{-}
denote the integers as a
\mathbb{Z}/2
-module, so that
1\in\mathbb{Z}/2
acts by multiplication by
-1
. We use
\mathbb{Z}
to denote the integers considered as a trivial
\mathbb{Z}/2
-module.

Lemma 7.3
.

Let
P
be a connected,
3
-coconnected CW complex with
\pi_{1}(P)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2
,
\pi_{2}(P)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
, and nontrivial
k
-invariant
0\neq k_{P}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2,\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}/2\oplus 0
.
Then we have isomorphisms

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{2}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{proj}_{1}}

Proof.

Since
P
is
3
-coconnected,
\pi_{3}(P)=\pi_{4}(P)=0
.
Thus
H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])\cong\Gamma(H_{2}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]))\cong\Gamma(\pi_{2}(P))\cong\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z})
.
By
Lemma

6.8

we also have

\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z})\cong\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}^{-})\oplus\Gamma(\mathbb{Z})\oplus(\mathbb{Z}^{-}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}^{-}

and hence

\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}\Gamma(\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2.

Next we build the following model for
P
, where the
4
-skeleton is explicit. Let
\alpha\in\pi_{3}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}
be a generator and
Y:=\mathbb{RP}^{2}\cup_{\alpha}D^{4}
. Then
X:=Y\times\mathbb{CP}^{2}
has

\displaystyle\pi_{1}(X)\cong\mathbb{Z}/2;\quad\pi_{2}(X)\cong\pi_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2})\oplus\pi_{2}(\mathbb{CP}^{2})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z};\text{ and }\pi_{3}(X)=0.

Furthermore, the inclusion
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to Y
induces an isomorphism

H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\pi_{2}(\mathbb{RP}^{2}))\xrightarrow{\cong}H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\pi_{2}(Y))

sending the
k
-invariant of
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
to the
k
-invariant of
Y
. Note that the first
k
-invariant of
\mathbb{RP}^{2}
is nontrivial; if not, there would be a retraction of the inclusion
\mathbb{RP}^{2}\to\mathbb{RP}^{\infty}
over the
3
-skeleton, i.e. there would be a retraction
\mathbb{RP}^{3}\to\mathbb{RP}^{2}
, which by a cup product argument in
\mathbb{Z}/2
-cohomology cannot exist.

Using the projection
X\to Y
, we have a map
H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\pi_{2}(X))\to H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\pi_{2}(Y))
sending the
k
-invariant of
X
to the
k
-invariant of
Y
.
It follows that the
k
-invariant of
X
is nontrivial.
So it follows that up to homotopy equivalence we can build
P
from
X
by attaching cells of dimensions
5
and higher. This finishes the construction of
X
.

By the Künneth theorem we have

H_{4}(X;\mathbb{Z})\cong H_{4}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{2}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\oplus H_{0}(Y;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}\oplus 0\oplus\mathbb{Z}\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}.

Hence
H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z})
is some quotient of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. We will now show that it is in fact isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
.

Consider the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her(H_{2}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])^{\dagger})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Her(H^{2}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])),}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}\circ\Upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ev^{*}}

where the map
\Theta_{P}
is given by
x\mapsto\big((\alpha,\beta)\mapsto\langle\beta,\alpha\cap x\rangle\big)
.
The diagram commutes as in
[
33
]
*Lemma 10.
Here we know that
\ev^{*}
is injective by
Corollary

5.2
. We also know from
Proposition

6.15
, and the fact that
\Upsilon
is an isomorphism, that the kernel of the map
\mathcal{B}_{H_{2}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}\circ\Upsilon
equals the torsion in
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
. Here we used the fact that
H_{2}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])\cong\pi_{2}(P)\cong\mathbb{Z}^{-}\oplus\mathbb{Z}
is free as an abelian group. Thus we know that the composition
\Theta_{P}\circ\varphi_{P}
is precisely the quotient by the torsion subgroup. Therefore
\Theta_{P}
has domain a quotient of
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
and image isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}^{2}
. Hence, it follows that
H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z})\cong\mathbb{Z}^{2}
, as claimed.
∎

Lemma 7.4
.

For
\pi=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism with
w(T)=1
, and let
v=wv^{\prime}
.
Let
B
be a connected,
3
-coconnected CW complex with
\pi_{1}(B)=\pi
,
\pi_{2}(B)\cong I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v}
, and
k
-invariant given by
0\neq k_{B}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\cong H^{3}(\pi;I\pi)\leq H^{3}(\pi;I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v})
. Let
P
be a CW complex as in
Lemma

7.3
. Then there exist maps
P\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}P
such that the composition is a self-homotopy equivalence of
P
.

Proof.

We will define the map
f\colon P\to B
, and will sketch the construction of the map
B\to P
.
Let
\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{i}\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\xrightarrow{p}\mathbb{Z}/2
be the inclusion and the projection maps.

We begin the construction of a map
f\colon P\to B
.
Define the module homomorphism
f_{1}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{-}\to I\pi
determined by
1\mapsto 1-T
, and the homomorphism
f_{2}\colon\mathbb{Z}\to I\pi^{v}
determined by
1\mapsto 1+T
. To show that
i\colon\pi_{1}(P)\to\pi_{1}(B)
and
(f_{1},f_{2})\colon\pi_{2}(P)\to\pi_{2}(B)
induce a map
P\to B
, note that since both
P
and
B
are connected and
3
-coconnected, to determine a map
P\to B
, it suffices to exhibit homomorphisms
\pi_{j}(P)\to\pi_{j}(B)
for
j=1,2
that respect the
k
-invariants.
More precisely, we have to show that

i^{*}k_{B}=(f_{1},f_{2})_{*}k_{P}\in H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}(I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v})).

For this, since both
k
-invariants are nontrivial and live in a group isomorphic to
\mathbb{Z}/2
, it suffices to show that the maps
i^{*}\colon H^{3}(\pi;I\pi)\to H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}I\pi)
and
(f_{1})_{*}\colon H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}^{-})\to H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}I\pi)
are isomorphisms. Here we used the fact that
k_{B}
lies in
H^{3}(\pi;I\pi)\leq H^{3}(\pi;I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v})
. We have a diagram as follows from dimension shifting using the Bockstein homomorphism and the sequence
0\to I\pi\to\mathbb{Z}\pi\to\mathbb{Z}\to 0
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\pi;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(\pi;I\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}I\pi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i^{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

To conclude that the horizontal maps are isomorphisms we used that
\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}\pi
is a free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
-module, and
H^{k}(\mathbb{Z}/2;Q)=0
for every free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
-module
Q
. Similarly we can compute directly that
H^{i}(\pi;\mathbb{Z}\pi)=0
for
i=2,3
.
The diagram commutes by naturality of the Bockstein map. That the left vertical map is an isomorphism is a straightforward computation in group cohomology, which can be performed by comparing standard free resolutions of
\mathbb{Z}
.
We deduce that the right vertical map
i^{*}
is an isomorphism as well.

It remains to show that
(f_{1})_{*}
is an isomorphism. For this consider the commutative diagram of
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
-modules:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{-}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}I\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}\mathbb{Z}\pi}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 1-T}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}

From this we obtain the following commuting diagram, where again the horizontal Bockstein maps are isomorphisms because both
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
and
\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}(\mathbb{Z}\pi)
are free
\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]
-modules.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z}^{-})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{2}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{3}(\mathbb{Z}/2;\Res^{\pi}_{\mathbb{Z}/2}I\pi).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(f_{1})_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}

Thus
(f_{1})_{*}
is an isomorphism and so
i
together with
(f_{1},f_{2})
induces a map
f\colon P\to B
.

Now we sketch the construction of a map
g\colon B\to P
.
Define an equivariant maps
g_{1}\colon I\pi\to\mathbb{Z}^{-}
given by sending
1-T
to
1
and
1-t
to
0
, and define
g_{2}\colon I\pi^{v}\to\mathbb{Z}
by sending
1+T
to
1
and
1-t
to
0
. A very similar argument to that in the construction of the map
f\colon P\to B
, which we omit, shows that
p
and
(g_{1},g_{2})
induces a map
B\to P
.
It is straightforward to check that the composition is the identity on
\pi_{1}
and
\pi_{2}
, and hence, since
P
is
3
-coconnected and a CW complex, by Whitehead’s theorem the composition
P\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}P
is a self-homotopy equivalence of
P
.
∎

Lemma 7.5
.

For
\pi=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism with
w(T)=1
and let
v=wv^{\prime}
.
Let
B
be a connected,
3
-coconnected CW complex with
\pi_{1}(B)=\pi
,
\pi_{2}(B)\cong I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v}
, and
k
-invariant given by
0\neq k_{B}\in\mathbb{Z}/2\cong H^{3}(\pi;I\pi)\leq H^{3}(\pi;I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v})
. Then the kernel of the map
\varphi_{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
contains a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup that injects into
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
under the change of coefficients map

\red_{2}^{B}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]).

Proof.

We first show that the kernel of the map
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
contains a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup. Let
P
be a CW complex as in
Lemma

7.3
. By
Lemma

7.4
, there exist maps
P\xrightarrow{f}B\xrightarrow{g}P
such that the composition is a self-homotopy equivalence.
Then we have the following commutative diagram, from considering the latter maps as well as the change of coefficients map.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{P}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{*}}

The composition
g\circ f
is a homotopy equivalence, and therefore the induced map
g_{*}\circ f_{*}
, which is the left vertical composition, is an isomorphism. It follows that the left vertical map
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])\xrightarrow{f_{*}}\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
is injective. The top right horizontal arrow was considered in
Lemma

7.3
and we know that the kernel, which we denote by
K
, is precisely the
\mathbb{Z}/2
summand in
\mathbb{Z}^{2}\oplus\mathbb{Z}/2\cong\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
. Note that in this map we do not see a contribution from
w
, since
w(T)=1
. As
K
is a summand of
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
, it maps nontrivially to
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(P;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
, which then maps also nontrivially to
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
since the map
f_{*}
is injective. By the commutativity of the diagram, we see that
K
must also map nontrivially to
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})
, along the central vertical map
f_{*}
. By definition
K
maps trivially to
H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
under the composition
f_{*}\circ\varphi_{P}
. Therefore, by commutativity of the diagram, the image
f_{*}(K)
in
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})
, which we know is nontrivial, must map trivially to
H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
. In other words the kernel of the map
\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w})
contains a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup as claimed. Moreover, we have shown that this subgroup maps nontrivially to
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\mathbb{Z}/2])
.

Let
\widehat{B}
be the covering of
B
with respect to the projection
\pi_{1}(B)=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2\to\mathbb{Z}/2
, and let
\widetilde{B}
denote the universal cover. We consider the fibration
\widetilde{B}\to\widehat{B}\to S^{1}
and the associated Leray–Serre spectral sequence
H_{p}(S^{1};H_{q}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{Z})^{w})\Rightarrow H_{p+q}(\widehat{B};\mathbb{Z}^{w})\cong H_{p+q}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})
. We have that
\pi_{3}(\widetilde{B})=0\to H_{3}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{Z})
is surjective by the Hurewicz theorem, and hence
H_{1}(S^{1};H_{3}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{Z}))=0
. From the spectral sequence we see that
E^{2}_{0,4}=\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})
is an isomorphism, where the codomain is the homology of the total space. This isomorphism is preserved under tensoring with
\mathbb{Z}
, and is the left vertical map in the commuting diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}(\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}[\mathbb{Z}/2]^{w})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B;\mathbb{Z}^{w}).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{B}}

The kernel of the right vertical map
\varphi_{B}
has a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup, as needed, since this is true for the kernel of the bottom horizontal map by our previous argument. Repeating this argument with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
coefficients shows that this subgroup of
\ker\varphi_{B}
is mapped injectively to
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
under the change of coefficients map. This completes the proof of
Lemma

7.5
.
∎

Now we deduce the fact corresponding to
Lemma

7.5
for the Postnikov
2
-type
B_{M}
of a closed
4
-manifold
M
with fundamental group
\pi=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
and
\pi_{2}(M)
stably isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v}
.

Corollary 7.6
.

For
\pi=\mathbb{Z}\times\mathbb{Z}/2
, let
w\colon\pi\to C_{2}
be a homomorphism with
w(T)=1
and let
v=wv^{\prime}\colon\pi\to C_{2}
.
Let
M
be a closed
4
-manifold with fundamental group
\pi
and orientation character
w
.
Assume further that
\pi_{2}(M)
is stably isomorphic to
I\pi\oplus I\pi^{v}
. Let
B_{M}:=P_{2}(M)
be the Postnikov
2
-type of
M
. Then the kernel of the map
\varphi_{B_{M}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}^{w})
contains a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup that injects into
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
under the change of coefficients map

\red_{2}^{M}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]).

Proof.

Let

M^{\prime}:=M\#\pm\mathbb{CP}^{2}.

We have maps
\iota
and
\rho
defined by

B_{M}:=P_{2}(M)\xrightarrow{\iota}P_{2}(M\vee S^{2})\simeq P_{2}(M^{\prime})=:B_{M^{\prime}}\xrightarrow{\rho}P_{2}(M),

where
\rho
collapses the
S^{2}
wedge-summand, and whose composition is homotopic to the identity.
This induces a sequence of homomorphisms

\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\iota_{*}}\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{Z}\pi)\xrightarrow{\rho_{*}}\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi),

whose composition is the identity. It follows that
\iota_{*}
is injective.
Similarly,

\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])

is injective, and we have a map

\rho_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])\xrightarrow{}\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]).

Claim
.

The kernel of
\varphi_{B_{M}}
contains a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup that maps injectively under the reduction of coefficients map
\red_{2}^{M}
if and only if the kernel of
\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}
contains a
\mathbb{Z}/2
subgroup that maps injectively under
\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}
.

The only if direction is easier, and we prove this first.
By naturality of
\varphi
and since
\iota_{*}
is injective, if
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is an order two element in the kernel of
\varphi_{B_{M}}
, then
\iota_{*}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
is an element of order two in the kernel of
\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}\colon\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{Z}\pi)\to H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{Z}^{w})
.
Since
\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
is injective, the image of
x
under the reduction of coefficients map
\red_{2}^{M}
is nontrivial if and only if this is true for
\iota_{*}(x)
with respect to
\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}
, which can be seen from the following commuting diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{Z}\pi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\red_{2}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}

This completes the proof of the only if direction of the claim.

Conversely, we start with an element
x\in\ker\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}\subseteq\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
of order two,
and assume that the image
\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}(x)\in\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
of
x
is nontrivial.
Then by naturality
\rho_{*}(x)\in\mathbb{Z}^{w}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}\pi}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{Z}\pi)
lies in the kernel of
\varphi_{B_{M}}
and since
\rho_{*}
is a homomorphism
\rho_{*}(x)
has order at most two. We need to show that
\rho_{*}(x)
has order exactly two, and that
\red_{2}^{M}(\rho_{*}(x))
is nontrivial.

For
N=M,M^{\prime}
, let
\varphi_{B_{N}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{N};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])\to H_{4}(B_{N};\mathbb{F}_{2})

be the version of
\varphi_{B_{N}}
with
\mathbb{F}_{2}
-coefficients.
We assert for the moment that
\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
restricts to an isomorphism

\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|\colon\ker\varphi_{B_{M}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\ker\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}.

Since
\rho_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|\circ\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|=\operatorname{Id}_{\ker\varphi_{B_{M}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
, we deduce that
\rho_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
restricts to an isomorphism

\rho_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|\colon\ker\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\ker\varphi_{B_{M}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}.

Since
\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}(x)
is assumed nontrivial, it follows that
\rho_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}(x))
is nontrivial. Observe that
\rho^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}_{*}(\red_{2}^{M^{\prime}}(x))=\red_{2}^{M}\circ\rho_{*}(x)
by naturality of reduction of coefficients.
Since the former is nontrivial, so is the latter, i.e.
\rho_{*}(x)
has nontrivial image under the reduction of coefficients map. In particular
\rho_{*}(x)
is nontrivial, and so it has order exactly two.
Thus to prove the if direction of the claim, it remains to prove that
\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|
is an isomorphism, which we do next.

For both
B=B_{M}
and
B=B_{M^{\prime}}
, we consider the fibration
\widetilde{B}\to B\to B\pi
, where
\widetilde{B}
denotes the universal cover, and the associated Leray–Serre spectral sequence
H_{p}(B\pi;H_{q}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{F}_{2}))\Rightarrow H_{p+q}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2})
. We have
E^{2}_{0,4}\cong\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
. Since
H_{1}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{F}_{2})=0=H_{3}(\widetilde{B};\mathbb{F}_{2})
, several differentials are necessarily trivial. In particular, there is no
d_{2}
- or
d_{4}
-differential with image in
E^{2}_{0,4}
, so we have homomorphisms as follows, which decompose
\varphi_{B}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
.

(7.7)

\displaystyle\varphi_{B}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\colon\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])

\displaystyle\cong E^{2}_{0,4}\xrightarrow{\cong}E^{3}_{0,4}\xrightarrow{-/\operatorname{Im}d_{3}}E^{4}_{0,4}\xrightarrow{\cong}E^{5}_{0,4}\xrightarrow{-/\operatorname{Im}d_{5}}E^{6}_{0,4}

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\cong}E^{\infty}_{0,4}\hookrightarrow H_{4}(B;\mathbb{F}_{2}).

We will investigate the
d_{3}
- and
d_{5}
-differentials presently.
First, we have
d_{3}
-differentials as shown below, with a commuting diagram with exact rows by naturality of the spectral sequence with respect to
\iota
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker{d_{3}^{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{5}(\pi;\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker{d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{5}(\pi;\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{2}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2})).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}}

Here the right-most isomorphism uses that
H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2})
and
H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2})
are stably isomorphic.
It follows that the induced map
\ker d_{3}^{M}\xrightarrow{\cong}\ker d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism.
Next we return to the analysis of
\varphi_{B_{M}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
and
\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
from (
7.7
).
Both maps are visible in the following commutative diagram, where the straight and the diagonal rows are exact. We already showed that
\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
is injective.
The fact that moreover

\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}
restricts to an isomorphism

\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}|\colon\ker\varphi_{B_{M}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\xrightarrow{\cong}\ker\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}.

follows from chasing this diagram.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker{d_{3}^{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker{d_{3}^{M}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ker{d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\infty}_{0,4}(M)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{3}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\coker{d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\infty}_{0,4}(M^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{5}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{M}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cong}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{5}^{M^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}}

To chase the diagram, let
x\in\ker\varphi_{B_{M^{\prime}}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
. By assumption this maps to
0
in
H_{4}(B_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2})
, and hence by injectivity of the bottom right horizontal map goes to
0
in
E^{\infty}_{0,4}(M^{\prime})
. Thus the image of
x
in
\coker{d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}}
lifts to
\ker d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}
and hence to
\ker d_{3}^{M}
. The image of this lift in
\coker d_{3}^{M}
maps by exactness of the diagonal top row to
0
in
H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2})
, and also by exactness of the top straight row lifts to
y\in\ker\varphi_{B_{M}}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}\subseteq\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
. By commutativity
\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(y)-x
maps to zero in
\coker d_{3}^{M^{\prime}}
and so lifts to
H_{3}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M^{\prime}};\mathbb{F}_{2}))
and hence to
H_{3}(\pi;H_{2}(\widetilde{B}_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}))
. Use the image of this lift in
\mathbb{F}_{2}\otimes_{\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi]}H_{4}(B_{M};\mathbb{F}_{2}[\pi])
to alter
y
to
y^{\prime}
with
\iota_{*}^{\mathbb{F}_{2}}(y^{\prime})=x
. By exactness we still have that
